Abstract: Scene Graph Generation (SGG) produces structured scene representations, yet its training-time backdoor risk remains unexplored. Unlike classification, SGG exposes a pair-level attack surface that enables selective corruption of relations involving a designated object category. However, because SGG largely relies on localized evidence, globally placed triggers may face difficulty in manipulating such predictions. Recent SGG architectures increasingly incorporate global context to improve scene modeling, which may in turn facilitate such attacks. In this paper, we systematically study this backdoor risk and investigate the role of the global context pathway in backdoor behavior. Concretely, we first instantiate this attack with an effective method, and propose a three-axis evaluation protocol to characterize backdoor behavior. Experiments on Visual Genome with representative open-vocabulary and closed-set SGG models demonstrate the effectiveness of the attack. Mechanistic analyses reveal that the explicit global context pathway serves as a major carrier of the backdoor effect across the evaluated SGG models. In particular, replacing the poisoned global context representation with its clean counterpart during inference sharply suppresses the attack, yielding 100% and 97% reductions in attack success for the OvSGTR family and RelationFormer, respectively. Moreover, training-time interventions in the OvSGTR family show that suppressing trigger signals in this pathway either reduces attack success or substantially degrades utility. Together, these findings suggest that explicit global context designs may pose a backdoor risk in SGG architectures.

</details>

Abstract: A deployable text-to-image diffusion model must hold several capabilities at once, such as instruction following, text rendering and human preference, and must sample in a few steps. Capabilities and few-step sampling are built in separate stages: a full-step model first consolidates capabilities through multi-teacher on-policy distillation, then serves as a fixed teacher for step distillation. Every full-step model we evaluate scores lower once compressed, and the second stage cannot begin until the first finishes. We introduce StepX-OPD, which merges the two stages: one student acquires every capability while learning to sample in a few steps, so frozen specialists supervise it on the states its own rollout visits rather than on a longer trajectory it never runs. Because a decomposition of the rollout's KL weights velocity mismatch most heavily near the clean end, we also regress the student's velocity on the low-noise part of the rollout. Separately, we find that capability integration makes models write prompt words into images on prompts that never asked for text, and we give a variant that lowers it. We evaluate on SD3.5-Medium and FLUX.1-dev across instruction following, text rendering, image quality and human preference. On both architectures, StepX-OPD attains the highest normalized average score among the four-step methods compared and exceeds the forty-step Flow-OPD model, and on SD3.5-Medium it trains with about 37% fewer GPU-hours than sequential DiffusionOPD followed by step distillation.

</details>

Abstract: Many fields of physical interest on the sphere, such as wind, the polarisation of the cosmic microwave background and the shear of weak gravitational lensing, are spin fields, whose values change phase when the local frame rotates. We extend spherical Fourier neural operators (SFNOs) to fields of any spin, through lifted spherical convolutions that are exactly rotationally equivariant. Lifting spin fields to Mackey functions on the rotation group provides the conceptual framework, while computation is performed exactly and efficiently through their harmonic representation and by appealing to sampling theorems on the sphere and rotation group. We construct spin SFNOs (S$^2$FNOs) from these building blocks. We demonstrate empirically that rotational equivariance holds to machine precision for these layers. We achieve state-of-the-art performance on existing benchmarks, and introduce new benchmarks on which spin-aware models outperform their scalar counterparts.

</details>

Abstract: Camera-LiDAR fusion repeatedly uses an extrinsic transform in projection, association, and recursive state estimation. We study whether a targetless correction should be admitted to a downstream consumer only after separate audits of correction quality, confidence ranking, and recursive risk. Our log-disjoint protocol evaluates these stages under synthetic extrinsic drift on Argoverse 2 and treats cross-dataset and odometry experiments as bounded diagnostics.
  On 27 test-split logs, single-frame Canny alignment is harmful in the mild tier. Multi-frame alignment improves severe-tier rotation by 2.52 degrees but worsens translation by 0.177 m; its declared joint error-magnitude improvement is also negative (-0.158, 95% CI [-0.223, -0.098]), so the rotation gain is not a six-degree-of-freedom correction success. A zero-injection check moves away from the true extrinsic, and solver-disagreement AUROC on selected splits does not yield a validated nonzero-coverage risk-coverage operating point.
  In a 15-log final system role, the locked conservative policy admits no candidate and equals Raw, whereas Always-correct increases trajectory error. These results support an auditable separation of correction, confidence, and recursive contamination; they do not establish a deployable calibration gate, positive odometry or SLAM improvement, map consistency, or closed-loop safety.

</details>

Abstract: Accurate lesion segmentation underpins automated dermoscopic analysis: masks enable measurements of asymmetry, border and size. Deployment in primary care or on mobile devices requires a compact network. Existing ultra-light Mamba U-Nets offer global context, but global-modeling cost depends on each Mamba block's resolution, while their local branches rely on a single square kernel. We introduce LCM-UNet, a 33,474-parameter U-shaped network that fuses every skip at its native resolution, restricts bidirectional Mamba to stages at one-sixteenth of the input resolution or coarser, and uses local convolutions at finer scales. A multi-basis reparameterizable adapter augments each local branch during training with dilated, horizontal and vertical depthwise kernels, then folds them into the host kernel before inference. Each skip connection also learns a stage-specific gain. Nine networks were retrained on the official ISIC 2017 and ISIC 2018 partitions under a harmonized protocol, with PH$^2$ reserved for external validation and ablations run on a development split. LCM-UNet achieved Dice scores of 88.62%, 89.73% and 90.46% on ISIC 2017, ISIC 2018 and PH$^2$, respectively, ranking first on both ISIC test sets and second on PH$^2$ among the compared models. In the native-resolution host, removing Mamba from the one-eighth-resolution decoder stage eliminated 63% of Mamba operations, corresponding to 5.3% of all operations, with a Dice change of $-0.03$ percentage points. The adapter improved Dice by 0.48 percentage points over a single host kernel without adding deployed parameters or operations. With latency so far measured only on a GPU, LCM-UNet is a candidate for resource-constrained deployment.

</details>

Abstract: Automated diabetic retinopathy (DR) grading from colour fundus photographs is held back by two compounding challenges: severe class imbalance across five severity grades and the heavy computational cost of large scale architectures in resource constrained clinical settings. We introduce DR-LiteNet, a dual branch hybrid convolutional neural network that tackles both problems at once. The CNN branch extracts 1,280 dimensional deep features through EfficientNetB0, while the handcrafted (HC) branch produces a compact 51 dimensional lesion descriptor capturing vessel density, exudate intensity, texture entropy, and HSV colour histograms. Concatenating both branches yields a 1,331-d hybrid embedding, on which Adaptive SMOTE (ADASYN) is applied during training to synthesise minority class feature vectors, correcting distributional skew without touching the image space. A two phase training protocol first warms up the classification head on balanced features, then fine tunes the full network end to end. Grad CAM saliency maps deliver post-hoc clinical explainability. Tested on APTOS 2019 under five fold stratified cross validation, DR-LiteNet reaches 88.77% +/- 0.61 accuracy, a quadratic weighted kappa (QWK) of 0.884 +/- 0.010, and a macro F1 of 0.792 +/- 0.008 with only 4.69 M parameters beating ResNet50 (23.5 M) by DeltaQWK = 0.022 while staying practical on a single Google Colab T4 GPU. The source code of this project is available at: https://github.com/Nishi-Kanta-Paul/DR-LiteNet.

</details>

Abstract: Missing modality imputation aims to synthesize unobserved modalities from observed ones. Existing methods treat each modality as living on its own latent manifold, forcing the generator to learn a long, unstructured jump between them. We present SphereFlow, which takes a fundamentally different view: by projecting all modalities into a shared hyperspherical latent space via a frozen self-supervised encoder, cross-modal imputation reduces to short-range geometric transport between nearby points on the sphere. Realizing this idea with flow matching, however, exposes two geometric conflicts: the standard Gaussian noise source is catastrophically mismatched with the unit-norm data manifold, and the linear interpolation path departs the sphere surface into a region where the decoder has no training signal. We resolve both with simple, geometry-aware modifications: replacing the noise source with the observed-modality latent so that the velocity field learns only the cross-modal displacement, and training the decoder with a time-weighted loss that extends its domain to the near-surface region traversed by the flow. We prove that this data-as-source formulation reduces the worst-case off-manifold deviation from a quantity that grows with the latent dimension to a small, dimension-independent bound governed by inter-modality similarity. Experiments on BraTS multi-modal MRI imputation and CT-MRI translation demonstrate state-of-the-art generation fidelity across all settings, while downstream brain tumor segmentation with our imputed data closes roughly eighty percent of the gap to the full-modality oracle. Code is available at https://github.com/WTHH031230/SphereFlow.

</details>

Abstract: Instruction-based image editors still primarily operate at 1K, whereas practical editing increasingly requires faithful 2K/4K outputs. Dense high-resolution adaptation preserves the editing trajectory but incurs expensive target-source attention; editing at 1K followed by external refinement is cheaper but can separate detail restoration from the editing trajectory. We introduce FLASH, a native high-resolution editor based on sparse cross-resolution denoising. A frozen 1K branch provides a semantic and structural anchor, while a target-resolution branch reconstructs local detail within the same Transformer. An asymmetric topology retains full 1K attention, guides high-resolution queries with spatially aligned low-resolution noisy-target states, and confines high-resolution target-reference interaction to local Halo neighborhoods. Resolution-specific diffusion-state mapping and spatial YaRN adapt the pretrained editor to larger grids. An optional, training-free progressive path replaces early high-resolution denoising with a 1K prefix and completes refinement in the same editor. On VINS-4KEval, the direct model achieves the strongest structural fidelity among the compared methods at both 2K and 4K. A separate 4K forward benchmark with R = 1 records 4.2x faster total forward execution and 25.3x faster attention than dense target-reference attention. Progressive inference reduces end-to-end 4K latency by 30.5%, with a modest quality cost.

</details>

Abstract: The advancement of multimodal learning is heavily constrained by the scarcity and high annotation cost of high-quality instruction data. While raw videos offer an abundant and dynamic source of visual knowledge, transforming them into precise instruction data remains highly challenging. To bridge this gap, we present VidUEU-Agent, an automated pipeline that synthesizes instruction data from raw videos for multimodal understanding, editing, and unified tasks. VidUEU-Agent follows a four-stage pipeline: it performs global perception to filter low-quality shots, mines keyframes and multimodal signals, constructs metadata and routes the metadata to synthesize instruction samples. More importantly, our agent framework can autonomously iterate the data distribution based on downstream training results, thereby optimizing data quality, balance, and training effectiveness. Scaling this pipeline, we construct VidUEU-100K, a large-scale dataset of 100K training samples spanning multiple domains and tasks, providing a robust foundation for developing comprehensive multimodal capabilities. Extensive experiments demonstrate that fine-tuning baseline models on VidUEU-100K yields significant and consistent performance gains across diverse representative benchmarks, validating the effectiveness of our proposed agent framework and dataset.

</details>

Abstract: Natural human-robot interaction requires robots to interpret language and gestures within a 3D environment to support tasks such as pick-and-place and object retrieval. 3D Embodied Reference Understanding (3D-ERU) supports this by identifying the 3D location of a target object from a scene (point-cloud) containing a human pointing gesture and an accompanying language description. However, current 3D-ERU models lack alignment of cross-modal representations and incur high processing costs that increase with the point-cloud's size. To address this, we propose GVT3D, which employs an Embodied Fusion module that jointly models language, gesture, and vision inputs within a shared embedding space, promoting cross-modal alignment. We additionally introduce a foveation approach that selectively processes the point cloud within a coarse region around the estimated pointing direction to reduce computations. GVT3D achieves ~8% higher accuracy and foveation enables 2.95x faster execution while maintaining comparable accuracy, relative to prior 3D-ERU models.

Abstract: Visualizing multivariate scientific simulation data is essential for understanding complex physical phenomena, yet the massive scale of such data imposes a severe storage bottleneck in high-performance computing environments. Implicit neural representations (INRs) have emerged as a promising continuous alternative to traditional lossy compressors, but existing methods struggle to explicitly capture cross-variable interactions and to adapt to the diverse spectral characteristics of physical variables. To address these limitations, we propose SWAP-INR, an architecture for high-fidelity compression of multivariate, time-varying simulation data. SWAP-INR introduces two key components: (1) The switchable attention path (SWAP) module explicitly models cross-variable interactions through a cross-variable attention layer, then routes each variable to an attention or non-attention path by a deterministic, loss-driven criterion, and prunes the inactive layers for structural compression. (2) The adaptability amplification SIREN (A2-SIREN) replaces rigid frequency scaling with channel-wise learnable modulation, adapting to heterogeneous physical features with negligible overhead. Combined with post-training 16-bit quantization, SWAP-INR achieves state-of-the-art performance on small modular reactor (SMR) datasets and remains competitive on public physics simulation datasets, while maintaining a constrained memory footprint. Our code is https://github.com/hvcl/SWAP-INR.

</details>

Abstract: Recent advances in video modeling have driven substantial progress in world action models (WAMs). Existing approaches exploit pretrained video priors by decoding actions from latent representations or inferring them from generated videos. The underlying link is causal: robot actions produce changes in the scene, and video observations of these changes provide evidence for inferring the actions that produced them. However, existing training formulations do not by themselves ensure that actions and their predicted visual consequences are consistent, potentially limiting the use of pretrained capabilities. We treat video and action as paired modalities linked by this causal relationship and learn their correspondence through a unified training framework designed to better exploit pretrained video priors. Cycle WAM first augments video-action co-training with bidirectional conversion to reinforce cross-modal correspondence and recover forward and inverse dynamics. It then introduces a differentiable cycle-reconstruction objective to further couple the two mappings. The resulting model supports both forward and inverse dynamics, with the potential to improve performance through more effective use of pretrained video priors. Cycle WAM achieves state-of-the-art performance among comparable methods on LIBERO-Pro and substantially improves DreamZero on DROID. Comparisons with UWM characterize its inverse-dynamics performance and show sharper visual detail in the displayed forward-dynamics predictions.

</details>

Abstract: The edge detection technique is an essential step for aircraft skin milling in aviation manufacturing. Most of the current detection methods focus on traditionally defined edge extraction tasks but disregard the crucial systematic requirement of edge milling. In this paper, we proposed a novel edge detection framework for automatic edge milling of aircraft skins. First, an edge probability detector is proposed by the spatial tangent continuity (STC) to provide the essential reference. Second, we propose a hierarchical branch searching (HBS) method to hierarchically strip the desired milling edges from the raw point cloud, which consists of three graded progressive steps: branch backbone generation, branch extension, and branch pruning. We demonstrate the performance of the proposed method on both synthetic models and aircraft skin workpieces. The proposed method outperforms the other baselines and shows accurate edges for the edge milling task.

Abstract: Large Vision-Language Models (LVLMs) routinely name objects that are absent from the image. Latent steering can reduce this failure in one model forward pass, but a fixed gate treats early and late decoding steps by the same criterion. We observe that predictive entropy varies sharply within free-running generation, while states that commit to short answers occupy a narrow, confident band. We exploit this temporal mismatch by using uncertainty as a causally available, token-local control signal for an existing steering gate. We propose Entropy-Gated Dynamic Rectification (EGDR), which lowers the intervention threshold when entropy derived from the preceding step's already available logits is high and retains the calibrated operating point when it is low. On LLaVA-1.5-7B, the underlying static gate lowers CHAIR CS/CI from 53.00/15.30 to 32.00/14.30; replacing only its constant threshold with EGDR further lowers them to 29.00/13.60. At a matched 58% intervention rate, the corresponding static control obtains 30.50/14.00. The gain over the static gate holds on LLaVA-NeXT-7B and grows with the generation budget, while POPE scores and general multimodal capability remain effectively unchanged. EGDR requires no additional model forward pass and adds roughly 2% measured latency. Our code is available at https://github.com/alvi-uiu/EGDR.

</details>

Abstract: We introduce iMED, the first synchronized multi-endoscope dataset for robot-assisted minimally invasive surgery (RAMIS), designed to address a fundamental limitation in surgical vision benchmarking: the absence of independent held-out viewpoints. Existing surgical datasets capture single-trajectory sequences from a single endoscope, making it impossible to distinguish geometric generalization from photometric interpolation along a narrow forward-facing path. iMED provides 340 sequences ~170K synchronized timepoints with 4 views per timepoint) recorded simultaneously from two independent stereo endoscopes across ex vivo, postmortem, and live surgical settings, spanning 14 specimens with diverse anatomical regions and motion regimes. The dataset includes calibrated camera intrinsics, ArUco-based frame-wise pose estimates with uncertainty quantification, instrument segmentation masks, and rich clinical metadata. Using a train-on-one-endoscope, test-on-another evaluation protocol, we benchmark 23 state-of-the-art methods across rigid and deformable novel view synthesis, pose estimation, feature matching, and monocular depth estimation. Our experiments show that methods relying primarily on photometric supervision degrade substantially under this held-out-endoscope setting, while methods with explicit geometric regularization are more robust across views in our benchmark. Notably, large-scale foundation models transfer surprisingly well to surgical imagery, often outperforming domain-specific models, suggesting pitfalls in current fine-tuning protocols. iMED establishes a new evaluation protocol for geometric generalization in surgical vision.

</details>

Abstract: Repeated robotic monitoring requires localization across visits to the same environment, even if the environment changes. Such changes can substantially reduce persistent visual content, and repetitive structures can lead to incorrect cross-session correspondences. In this paper, we address visual multi-session registration of a new data recording (the query session) to a previously reconstructed reference map. Under substantial visual and structural changes, cross-session localization may be possible only for parts of the trajectory. In our approach, we therefore rely on a sparse set of reliable absolute pose constraints and combine them with within-session relative-pose information to register the complete trajectory to the reference map. We first estimate absolute alignment hypotheses through visual place recognition, local feature matching, and geometric verification. We then use sequence consensus to reject hypotheses that are inconsistent with a common query-to-reference alignment and retain the remaining poses as absolute anchors. These anchors are refined through featuremetric alignment against a Gaussian feature field and combined with within-session relative-pose constraints in a robust pose-graph optimization. We evaluate our approach on 15 cross-session experiments from the challenging HILTI26 construction-site dataset, covering substantial scene changes and difficult-to-match environments. Our method achieves 94.4% recall within 1m and 100.0% within 5m, outperforming independent image localization, which peaks at 65.3% and 70.7%, respectively, as well as direct registration of independently reconstructed Structure-from-Motion geometry. Our results show that sparse, reliable absolute anchors can provide accurate and complete trajectory registration even when reliable cross-session correspondences are available only for part of the query session.

</details>

Abstract: Moving Object Segmentation (MOS) detects and separates dynamic objects from the static background in a video, which plays a fundamental role in dynamic-scene understanding. Most MOS methods rely on pure visual motion cues alone, such as optical flow or point tracks, which is inherently ambiguous due to the compounded camera and object motions as well as motion-induced appearance changes, e.g. shadows and reflections. Naive fusion of additional information such as depth and semantics, however, cannot leverage their complementarity to the fullest extent. We therefore propose DynOSeg, a novel MOS approach that takes on a two-stage fusion structure. DynOSeg first combines a geometry-aware residual map and depth encoding into a geometric representation of motion, delineating camera motions and object dynamics. We then further incorporate semantic features to refine object understanding. We have additionally curated a diverse training corpus including CARLA-MOS, a new synthetic urban-driving dataset for MOS training. Our experiments show that DynOSeg consistently outperforms published state-of-the-art MOS methods across a diverse range of datasets. Our code and pretrained models can be found at https://github.com/ACFR-RPG/DynOSeg

</details>

Abstract: We propose PadLoc, a training-free localization back-end that places a moving sensor within a known floor plan. Floor plans are cheap, persistent, and robust to appearance change, but recent learned-depth methods localize against them by filtering per-frame depth through dense $(x,y,θ)$ pose grids. PadLoc keeps the swappable depth front-end and replaces the dense grid back-end with sparse geometric matching: dominant wall orientations define a small pose-candidate set, masked normalized cross-correlation scores observed free space against the plan, and a moving-pose Gaussian-mixture Bayes filter plus consensus readout decodes the trajectory. Holding each competitor's depth fixed, PadLoc matches or beats UnLoc's forward filter and the F3Loc filter at every Gibson trajectory length, and UnLoc's offline refinement from $T=35$ on; it exceeds Z-FLoc's published results with a shared MapAnything front-end. On Bonn LiLocBench, PadLoc improves pooled SR@1m over UnLoc by 23-32 pp under wheel odometry and 35-43 pp with ground-truth motion; with full-FOV LiDAR plus wheel odometry the same readout reaches 100% SR@1m at 9-10 cm median error on the static sequence. On the Hilti-Trimble Localization Challenge PadLoc localizes a camera-only fisheye rig on construction floors hundreds of metres across at 0.90-0.98 online SR@2m on three of five sessions; adding learned wall isolation, applied to every session, covers all five at 0.67-0.92 with the localizer unchanged throughout. PadLoc thus matches or outperforms dense grid back-ends while preserving sensor modularity and a compact state that does not grow with trajectory length.

</details>

Abstract: Unified multimodal models (UMMs) integrate image generation and visual understanding, but are existing single-pathway unlearning methods sufficient to suppress target concepts across both? In this work, we study cross-pathway concept unlearning. Our goal is to prevent a model from both generating images of a target concept and identifying it in input images, while preserving other capabilities. To this end, we introduce UUBench, a diagnostic benchmark covering 30 target concepts and 60 semantically related neighboring concepts. Experiments on representative UMMs show that the evaluated single-pathway methods achieve limited joint forgetting: generation-side editors strongly suppress target generation but yield limited forgetting in visual understanding, while understanding-side baselines struggle to achieve substantial forgetting even in their own pathway. Their direct composition also yields limited joint forgetting. We therefore propose UniForget, achieving joint forgetting rates of 80.0% on BAGEL-7B-MoT and 92.7% on Janus-Pro-7B, outperforming representative baselines. Further analysis reveals trade-offs between joint forgetting and capability preservation.

</details>

Abstract: Reliable evaluation of visual 3D reconstruction and depth estimation in vegetation-rich outdoor environments is limited by the scarcity of geometric ground-truth data and the cost of complete scanner surveys. We present a LiDARbased reference-generation pipeline for evaluating the zero-shot performance of feed-forward visual-geometry models alongside conventional structure-from-motion (SfM) methods. The pipeline reuses camera images, LiDAR scans, and reference poses from existing multi-sensor localization datasets to generate per-view depth maps and visible reference point clouds. To address visibility errors in accumulated LiDAR scans, we introduce the LargeDepth-Gap Visibility Score (LDGVS) for point-wise visibility filtering and automatic scale selection in Hidden Point Removal(HPR). Experiments on TartanAirV2 and TartanGround show that, compared with Poisson surface reconstruction, which is an approach suited to completed scan surveys, the combined HPR and LDGVS filtering retains approximately three times the point density at comparable absolute relative error. We also apply the pipeline to the BotanicGarden dataset to systematically compare depth estimation and 3D reconstruction methods. Feedforward methods produce dense depth predictions efficiently, but their 3D reconstructions can contain duplicated structures. Conventional SfM generally produces more coherent geometry, but its reconstructions remain sparse and more prone to failure in challenging vegetated scenes. The benchmark toolkit and datasets will be released.

</details>

Abstract: Large vision-language models (LVLMs) can process interleaved text and multiple images in a single context, but how they internally identify the image relevant to a question remains poorly understood. We study this grounding step as in-context image retrieval and introduce ICIR-MCQ, a controlled probe for head-level analysis of image retrieval. Using each head's attention mass over image-token spans, we first identify sparse pointer heads that reliably point to the target image. These heads transfer beyond the probe: a single pointer head is competitive with external vision-language retrievers on a naturalistic multi-image retrieval benchmark, showing that LVLMs contain a strong internal image retrieval signal. Attention alone, however, shows where a head reads from, not what its output passes on to later layers, so we introduce a complementary output-side analysis. For each head, we train a lightweight image retriever on its value-weighted output and use it to identify writer heads, whose outputs contain enough information to identify the target image. Surprisingly, the pointer and writer criteria select substantially different head sets, with only 35 to 47 heads overlapping among the top-100 heads under each criterion. Targeted set-partition ablations show that the heads most important for standard multi-image inference are concentrated in this pointer-writer intersection, while heads selected by only one criterion show less consistent separation from random-head controls. The most consistent causal effects arise from heads selected by both the attention-based pointer and output-side writer criteria. Code and data: https://github.com/parkquasar/Image-Retrieval-Heads.

</details>

Abstract: High-resolution land-cover maps support ecological monitoring, but consistent historical time series remain scarce because aerial imagery varies across years and sensors. We developed a dual-track workflow to produce temporally coherent 1-m land-cover maps from National Agricultural Imagery Program (NAIP) imagery without historical training samples. For higher-quality imagery from 2009-2017, we used a pretrained U-Net/ResNet-34 model and refined boundaries with the Segment Anything Model (SAM). For lower-quality imagery from 2004-2008, we reconstructed land cover through an NLCD-based spatiotemporal bridging and label back-casting pipeline. Evaluation across six counties in North Carolina and Pennsylvania yielded overall accuracies of 0.887 (2014), 0.886 (2017), and 0.733 (2004). Structure, Water, Wetland, and Cropland consistently achieved high F1-scores. The workflow integrates GeoAI and multiple data sources to address temporal heterogeneity in historical aerial imagery, supporting high-resolution land-change analysis and landscape planning across the conterminous United States.

Abstract: Learning a universal action representation from video requires modeling dynamics that are consistent across different executions of the same action yet invariant to changes in embodiment and appearance. Existing latent action models infer actions from visual transitions, but their representations often entangle the underlying action with irrelevant visual variations, limiting transfer across embodiments. We introduce DreamX, which repurposes a pretrained video diffusion model as a universal latent action learner. Our approach extracts compact latent actions from the video model's rich spatiotemporal features, compressing each latent video frame by $\boldsymbol{300\times}$ ($\boldsymbol{1{,}200 \rightarrow 4}$ tokens). In addition, by leveraging the video model's generative prior, these latent actions capture action-relevant dynamics disentangled from visual appearance. The resulting latent space serves as a shared action interface across embodiments (human and robot), environments, and action parameterizations. On LARY-Bench, DreamX achieves state-of-the-art performance among latent action models. Moreover, actions encoded in one setting remain effective in another: the latent space supports any-to-any video transfer and zero-shot action transfer across diverse embodiments and environments. Furthermore, this shared interface also enables policy learning in a compact latent action space, reducing the computational cost of video-diffusion-based policies.

</details>

Abstract: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) allow social scientists to analyze video at scale, but researchers must decide which parts of a video to provide as input. We use the term representation validity to examine how text, audio, video, and combined inputs affect measurement and subsequent analysis. We compare input representations across six open-weight MLLMs and three human-annotated video datasets. No representation consistently performs best. On seven news-related constructs, native-video performance varies little across models (macro-F1 = .70-.77), while extracted-text performance ranges from .52 to .80. Three models perform better with text and three with video. Combining inputs rarely improves performance. Higher classification accuracy also does not reliably produce estimates closer to those based on human codes. The highest-F1 representation best reproduces human-coded regression coefficients in 17 of 42 comparisons. Even when each model uses its highest-F1 deployable representation, 45% of sample-prevalence estimates differ from the human-coded benchmark by more than ten percentage points. Input representation therefore requires validation for both the construct being measured and the quantity estimated from the resulting labels.

</details>

Abstract: Video Reasoning Segmentation (VRS) aims to infer and segment target objects specified by reasoning queries in videos. Existing methods typically first infer the target and generate a few segmentation tokens for it, which are then fed into a mask decoder for mask prediction. However, such segmentation tokens tend to be both semantically monotonous and spatially ambiguous, making them largely insufficient to represent the target to guide mask prediction. In this paper, we propose an explicit visual object grounding framework for VRS. Specifically, we first propose a spatial-aware prompt generation and refinement scheme, in which we query a multimodal large language model to generate explicit spatial prompts (i.e., bounding box and point set) for the target and then refine them through multi-step dialogue. Furthermore, we introduce a query diversification and alignment module to generate auxiliary queries that describe the same target from different perspectives. We then enforce cross-query consistency between their predicted spatial prompts to alleviate the training bias caused by query limitations. Finally, we select several reliable frames using a prompt-based keyframe selection strategy to support mask decoding and propagation. Extensive experiments on eight datasets demonstrate that our method significantly outperforms state-of-the-art methods. The code is available at https://github.com/viscom-tongji/EVOG.

</details>

Abstract: Audio-based piano transcription is hampered by the sustain pedal and by noisy or missing soundtracks, problems that video-only transcription avoids, since a top-view video shows directly when each key is pressed and released. Most visual transcription networks, however, describe the whole keyboard with one global feature vector and must learn where each key is. In this paper, we propose KeyWise, a lightweight per-key architecture that exploits the known geometry of the keyboard instead of learning it. The rectified keyboard is divided into 88 equal tiles that serve as regions of interest (ROIs), and one small recurrent network, shared by all keys, follows each key over time. On PianoVAM, KeyWise reaches the onset accuracy of the state-of-the-art V2N (95.0% vs. 94.7% onset F1 at 50 ms and 97.8% vs. 97.0% at 100 ms) with 1.3 M instead of 28.2 M parameters, and it runs 4.7 times faster on the same GPU. At 100 ms, it is also significantly more accurate when the key releases (97.0% vs. 95.5% F1) or the velocities (89.4% vs. 84.5% F1) must be correct as well. On PianoYT, KeyWise pre-trained on PianoVAM reaches 69.4% onset F1 at 100 ms, comparable to the best published result, although it needs fine-tuning for new recording conditions.

</details>


### [189] [Bridging CLIP with DINO: Cross-Modal Information Maximization for Online Test-Time Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04489)

Abstract: Vision-language models such as CLIP enable zero-shot classification from arbitrary class names, but under test-time corruptions, their visual encoder projects distorted features onto incorrect textual anchors with high confidence: a phenomenon we term modality bias. Standard entropy minimization reinforces these overconfident errors, which creates a confirmation-bias loop and collapses the predicted label distribution. We propose an online test-time adaptation (TTA) method that breaks this loop at three levels. At the representation level, a frozen self-supervised backbone (e.g., DINO v2) is combined with CLIP through a cross-covariance logit ensemble. CLIP text prototypes are projected into the feature space of this text-free backbone, so the self-supervised branch classifies native image features and is less affected by the text bias of CLIP. At the optimization level, a gated test-time information-maximization objective pairs per-sample sharpening with batch-level diversity, while an adaptive gate excludes unreliable samples from the gradient. At the prediction level, an online logit adjustment corrects the residual class-distribution skew. CDIM outperforms the compared CLIP-based online TTA baselines on ImageNet-C, CIFAR10-C, and CIFAR100-C, and remains competitive in continual and mixed-domain TTA. Extensive analyses and ablation studies verify the contribution of each of its components. Our code is available at https://github.com/B1ueC1oud/CDIM

</details>

Abstract: Monocular depth estimation (MDE) has advanced rapidly with the emergence of foundation models such as Depth Anything. Their transformer-based architectures provide strong generalization across diverse scenes and domains, but also incur high computational and memory cost, making efficient deployment challenging. Post-Training Quantization (PTQ) provides an efficient and practical solution for model compression, yet low-bit PTQ remains challenging for MDE models. When applying PTQ to Depth Anything, we identify two key challenges: (i) independently minimizing the quantization error of the query and key projections fails to preserve the attention maps induced by their interaction, and (ii) quantization errors accumulate across layers, resulting in intermediate feature distribution shifts. To address these issues, we propose SPA-Q, a Structure-Preserving Adaptive PTQ framework for MDE, consisting of two main components. First, Attention-Preserving Calibration (APC) determines query and key quantization parameters by matching the full-precision attention distribution. Second, Channel-Wise Distribution Alignment (CWDA) learns channel-wise affine transformations to mitigate quantization-induced distribution shifts, and the learned parameters are absorbed into the weights after training. Experimental results show that SPA-Q consistently outperforms existing PTQ methods under 4-bit quantization, achieving an average 25.9% reduction in AbsRel and a 17.3% improvement in $δ_1$ across NYUv2 and KITTI datasets.

</details>

Abstract: Few-shot high-resolution range profile (HRRP) recognition is challenged by strong aspect-dependent variation and sparse novel-class support. We propose polarimetric scattering-structure contrastive learning (PSS-CL), which supplies an episodic HRRP encoder with a structured physical training view. Dominant local responses are represented by their soft affinities to canonical polarimetric mechanisms, normalized range locations, and energy contributions. A symmetric cross-view objective transfers this scattering structure to the learned HRRP representation, while episodic prototype learning supports novel-class inference without the physical branch. We conduct Random-aspect and Cross-aspect experiments on a six-class measured dataset. In Cross-aspect 1-, 3-, and 5-shot recognition, PSS-CL achieves 81.73%, 86.92%, and 90.98% mean class accuracy (MCA), respectively, outperforming the best competing results by 5.62%, 3.09%, and 3.56%.

</details>


### [192] [3DGS-VBench+ and 3DGSV-Assessor: A Multi-Dimensional Subjective Benchmark and a Reference-Flexible Objective Model for 3D Gaussian Splatting Video Quality Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04498)

Abstract: 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) enables real-time novel view synthesis (NVS), but its heavy storage makes compression essential, and both training and compression introduce representation-specific distortions. Since generative compression in many methods treats geometric and color attributes via different parameters, it can induce dimension-specific distortions, motivating multi-dimensional assessment. Moreover, real-world captures generally lack an aligned ground-truth video for a synthesized, continuous camera trajectory, leaving only sparse captured views as references. To enable multi-dimensional quality assessment of 3DGS videos with scarce aligned references, we first present 3DGS-VBench+, a large-scale subjective video quality assessment (VQA) benchmark comprising 3{,}036 3DGS reconstructions, each paired with a rendered video, across 11 real-world and 22 synthetic scenes, generated by 6 representative compression algorithms at systematically designed levels and annotated with 9{,}108 MOSs covering overall, geometry, and color quality. Based on 3DGS-VBench+, we propose 3DGSV-Assessor, a reference-flexible, large multimodal framework that scores all three dimensions in one generation pass and fuses a ranking-based reference cue into the frozen regressor through a bounded gate, supporting both reduced-reference (RR) and no-reference (NR) assessment. Extensive experiments demonstrate state-of-the-art correlation with human perception on 3DGS-VBench+ and strong zero-shot generalization to other NVS benchmarks. The dataset and code will be released at https://github.com/YukeXing/3DGSV-Assessor.

</details>

Abstract: Electroencephalography (EEG)-based visual decoding and reconstruction aims to recover visual information from non-invasive brain signals. But there are challenges due to the complex temporal-frequency characteristics of EEG signals and the difficulty of discrete denoising framework in mapping neural activity to meaningful visual representations. Here, we propose E2I-Flow, an EEG-to-image decoding and reconstruction framework that combines a novel EEG encoder for time-frequency representation learning from EEG features, and conditional flow matching for visual decoding. A stationary-wavelet frequency branch and a temporal-mixing branch are integrated through bidirectional cross-attention and adaptive fusion, with the resulting EEG representations aligned to ground-truth image embeddings using contrastive and mean-squared-error loss supervisions. The aligned EEG representation is then used as a condition for a Transformer-based flow-matching model to predict the corresponding visual embedding for image synthesis. Experimental results show strong zero-shot EEG-to-image retrieval and improved visual reconstruction performance, outperforming several state-of-the-art baselines.

</details>


### [194] [Learning to Falsify: Hierarchical Multiple Instance Learning for High Resolution AI-Generated Image Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04529)
*Haoran Chen,Daiqi Gao,Xinyuan Li,Yongwei Wang,Lei Zhu,Rui Cai,Jianfeng Dong*

Abstract: Recent advances in generative models have made synthetic images increasingly photorealistic, posing growing challenges to reliable and generalizable detection of AI-generated images. Existing methods typically rely on holistic image-level classification, which may overlook local artifacts, especially in high-resolution images. To address this limitation, we formulate this task as a falsification-driven multiple instance learning problem, where a single fake patch can falsify the whole image, while a real image requires all patches to be authentic. Based on this formulation, we propose \textit{Learning to Falsify (LTF)}, a hierarchical multiple instance learning framework that models an image as multiple bags of instances across different spatial levels and aggregates their predictions under the falsification-inspired principle. We further introduce Cross-Shuffle during training to encourage the model to learn local artifact evidence rather than image-level shortcuts. Furthermore, we propose the \textit{Falsification-driven Test-time Aggregation Strategy (FATAS)}, a plug-and-play module that extends the same falsification-inspired principle to existing detectors by aggregating patch-level predictions. Extensive experiments demonstrate that LTF achieves strong generalization and robustness across multiple benchmarks, while FATAS substantially improves representative detectors in high-resolution scenarios without retraining. Code is available at https://github.com/HuiGuanLab/LTF.git

</details>


### [195] [BtsPruner: Role-Aware Visual Token Pruning for Large Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04537)

Abstract: Large Vision-Language Models (LVLMs) often process hundreds or thousands of visual tokens, resulting in substantial computational and memory overhead during inference. Existing training-free pruning methods typically estimate token importance using language-model attention, visual attention, or feature similarity. However, these methods treat visual tokens with uniform criteria and overlook that tokens may serve different roles during visual encoding. In this work, we investigate the evolution of visual token roles in LVLMs and observe that tokens progressively transition from local detail carriers to globally referenced representatives as encoding proceeds. Based on this observation, we propose BtsPruner, a training-free role-aware visual token pruning framework that preserves complementary token roles under a fixed budget. Specifically, BtsPruner first identifies the token-role transition point based on the evolution of attention concentration. It then discovers globally referenced representatives from later encoding stages and recursively retrieves local detail tokens from earlier stages to recover complementary fine-grained evidence. Extensive experiments across LLaVA-1.5, LLaVA-NeXT, Qwen-VL, InternVL, and CogVLM demonstrate that BtsPruner achieves superior performance retention under aggressive token reduction. On LLaVA-1.5-7B, BtsPruner preserves 91.6% of the dense model's performance using only 32 of 576 visual tokens (5.6%). In the high-resolution LLaVA-NeXT-7B setting, it achieves up to a 5.95x CUDA-latency speedup.

</details>


### [196] [A Universal Raw-IQ Radar Encoder for Cross-Sensor Human Sensing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04534)
*Jonas Leo Mueller,Dario Zanca,Sebastian Hoefler,Jan Petermann,Thomas Altstidl,Naga Venkata Sai Jitin Jami,Emmanuelle Salin,Bjoern M. Eskofier*

Abstract: Radar-based human sensing typically relies on end-to-end task-specific models trained on hand-chosen signal projections, which generalize poorly across unseen users, tasks, and hardware. We argue that the dominant signal processing choice, the micro-Doppler spectrogram, is a poor starting point for transfer because it keeps the Doppler axis, which carrier frequency rescales, and discards the angular axis, which carrier frequency leaves unchanged. We instead pretrain a convolutional beta-VAE without labels on clutter-removed raw radar measurements from four platforms spanning 60 and 77 GHz and 4 to 12 virtual antennas, aligned by a unified spectral interpolation grid. For every downstream benchmark the encoder stays frozen, and only a small supervised temporal classifier is trained on its embeddings. We compare against supervised models trained end to end on each benchmark and a pretrained radar foundation model across ten settings covering activity, gesture, sign-language, and gait recognition. When the test users and the radar are seen during training, our encoder is best on all five benchmarks. For users never seen in training, it leads on three of four benchmarks, by 4.8 to 23.2 points. On subject-disjoint gait recognition, it is best for each of the four held-out subjects and 8.3 points above the strongest baseline on average, even though its pretraining excluded every recording of this radar. Excluding the test radar from pretraining costs at most 5.3 points on any benchmark. Code is available at https://github.com/jonasmueler/UniversalRadarEncoder_WACV.

</details>


### [197] [Predicting Void Points in Self-Occluding Forest Volumes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04548)
*Benjamin Bachmayr,Mohamed Youssef,Oliver Bimber*

Method: 解析错误

Result: 解析错误

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: Synthetic Aperture Imaging (SAI) enables drones to observe forest canopies by warping and averaging multiple displaced views at a common depth. However, occlusion is suppressed rather than eliminated, causing poorly observed regions to appear occupied and leading to systematic biomass overestimation. The Deep Forest project cannot distinguish reconstruction artifacts from real vegetation because its training data relies on measured reflectance points, excluding empty space and preventing the network from learning explicit occupancy boundaries.
  This thesis addresses the problem by modifying training labels rather than the imaging process. Each simulated scene is rendered twice: as a 9x9 grid of top-down captures for focal-stack generation and as 550 depth-clipped slabs derived from scene geometry to provide occupancy labels, explicitly representing void space as a negative class. A Gazebo pipeline generates procedural forest stands containing 80-200 trees across three species. A pretrained Pix2Pix model translates rendered RGB images into a synthetic near-infrared band, and Light Field Rendering produces aligned focal stacks of 550 planes across three channels.
  Four architectures were evaluated on a held-out scene. The plain 2D U-Net achieved an IoU of 0.6290, outperforming the volumetric 3D U-Net (0.5707). Established reconstruction methods performed substantially worse: COLMAP achieved 0.3446 and DepthAnything3 0.2959, both after their respective optimal dilation sweeps. An RGB-input variant of the volumetric architecture achieved the highest IoU of 0.6514 without dilation or per-scene calibration. These results highlight the limitations of conventional reconstruction methods, which recover visible surfaces but struggle to infer occupancy within closed canopies.

</details>


### [198] [SEEK-VAU: Towards Evidence-Faithful Video Anomaly Understanding via Agentic Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04554)
*Minghe Wang,Weijun Zeng,Dapeng Luo,Xinyue Wang,Nong Sang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: 解析错误


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 解析错误

Method: 解析错误

Result: 解析错误

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: Most video anomaly understanding (VAU) systems still infer anomaly judgments from evidence collected in a feed-forward way under predefined rules, typically sampled frames, or events clustered from such frames. This protocol fails when the collected evidence is incomplete under imperfect predefined rules, because once reasoning begins the model can no longer search for the missing evidence. We address this limitation with SEEK-VAU, which reframes VAU as an agentic search-and-verify interaction protocol under a bounded search budget: instead of passively reasoning over pre-collected evidence, the policy actively gathers evidence across the three stages of an anomaly event chain (precursor, trigger, and aftermath) and verifies its sufficiency before finalization, turning event-chain completeness into an explicit design rather than an outcome of predefined rules. A bounded budget caps the cost of this search, but does not by itself teach the policy where to look or when to stop. To make this behavior learnable, we introduce evidence-faithful counterfactual verification (EFCV), which rewards selected evidence that remains supportive, compact, and necessary under counterfactual verification. We further introduce SEEK-Bench, featuring video-level episodes with temporal interval annotations, semantic QA and event-chain stage labels. Together, SEEK-VAU and SEEK-Bench establish a strong foundation for evidence-faithful and actively verifiable VAU.

</details>

Abstract: 3D semantic scene completion plays a critical role in enabling various downstream tasks within autonomous systems by estimating both missing geometric structures and semantic information in scene data. In this work, we introduce a unique neural framework that integrates state space modeling with diffusion-based generative approaches to enhance 3D point cloud semantic scene completion. We propose \textit{MPath Mamba} diffusion network, leveraging a skip-triple Mamba structure, dimensional decomposition residuals, and multi-directional dilations within multi-path data to efficiently process long-sequence data for feature extraction. The proposed method operates in the conditioned latent space of a variational autoencoder, enhanced by a state-space mechanism to improve 3D point cloud representation and reconstruction. Extensive experiments conducted on the SemanticKITTI benchmark demonstrate that our approach achieves highly competitive performance for 3D point cloud semantic scene completion.

</details>

Abstract: Coarse-to-fine image-to-point-cloud (I2P) registration risks discarding correct but ambiguous patch pairs, while retaining more candidates repeats fine matching over overlapping patches. SparseGraph-I2P aims to improve registration recall by increasing the inlier ratio at the pose solver, using sensor or monocular depth. A Coarse Hypothesis Graph combines appearance with depth-derived first- and second-order compatibility to select a fixed budget of patch pairs. Sparse Fine Matching decodes each queried pixel once and skips pixel--point comparisons covered by an ancestor patch paired with the same point-cloud region. With sensor depth, depth-aware filtering ranks correspondences by SC2 spectral support for a single EPnP solve. With monocular depth, depth or inverse depth is calibrated from correspondence geometry before filtering. SparseGraph-I2P achieves the highest registration recall and lowest latency among the compared methods on RGB-D Scenes V2 and 7-Scenes with sensor depth. With predicted depth, it retains the highest recall on RGB-D Scenes V2 at an additional calibration cost. Code will be released.

</details>

Abstract: Weakly supervised oriented object detection (WS-OOD) aims to reduce the annotation cost of rotated bounding boxes by leveraging cheaper annotations such as horizontal boxes or points. However, these weak annotations provide no explicit orientation information, forcing existing methods to infer object angles through indirect geometric consistency or equivariant constraints. Such indirect supervision often becomes ambiguous and unstable under real-world appearance variations, including illumination changes, color shifts, shadows, and background clutter. In this paper, we present a simple yet effective gradient-guided orientation learning strategy for WS-OOD. Motivated by the observation that the input image itself contains useful orientation priors, our method extracts dominant gradient-based orientation cues from object regions and converts them into pseudo-angle labels for auxiliary supervision, enabling the detector to learn orientation-aware representations without requiring rotated bounding box annotations or extra learnable parameters. Furthermore, since AP can mask substantial angular errors when spatial IoU remains high, we introduce an orientation-aware evaluation metric that incorporates angular discrepancies into the standard AP protocol. Extensive experiments on weakly supervised oriented detection benchmarks demonstrate that the proposed pseudo-angle supervision improves both detection performance and orientation estimation quality, while the proposed metric provides a more faithful assessment of angle prediction than conventional AP alone.

</details>

Abstract: A key challenge in colon capsule endoscopy (CCE) analysis is accurately identifying whether multiple images depict the same polyp, which is critical to avoid over-counting and unnecessary clinical interventions. Additionally, this prevents prolonged follow-up colonoscopies where endoscopists search for duplicate polyps without precise localization. This study presents a novel contrastive learning-based approach to polyp matching using a Siamese neural network architecture. The model was trained on a dataset from the Danish CareForColon2015 trial. The dataset comprises 5,103 image pairs of confirmed same polyp and 5,103 pairs of different polyps, annotated by experienced clinicians. The proposed model employs ResNet variants (ResNet18, ResNet34, ResNet50), DenseNet121 and EfficientNet B0/B3 as backbones and utilizes contrastive learning with cosine vector distance metric for classification. Experimental results demonstrate high classification accuracies of up to 92% and AUC values close to 0.98. These results suggest that automated polyp matching has the potential to reduce polyp over-counting, improve patient risk stratification, and reduce unnecessary follow-up colonoscopies.

</details>

Abstract: Dynamic hand gesture authentication (DHGA) is an emerging biometric technique that contains both physiological and behavioral identity characteristics. However, existing methods primarily rely on convolutional networks or manually designed motion representations, making it difficult to jointly capture fine-grained local identity differences and long-range behavioral dependencies during hand gesture execution. To address these challenges, we propose a two-stream identity representation learning framework, MambaHand Net, to combine CNNs with Mamba for DHGA. The proposed framework consists of a Local Identity Stream (LIS) and a Behavioral Sequential Stream (BSS). The LIS extracts frame-wise local identity cues with a convolutional network, while the BSS preserves spatial tokens from intermediate visual features and constructs first-order and second-order temporal difference sequences to model multi-order behavioral variations. Moreover, the bidirectional Mamba is incorporated into the BSS to model semantic motion across different spatial locations, enabling efficient long-range modeling of hand gesture execution habits. Finally, attention-guided spatial token aggregation and a two-stream fusion strategy are used to integrate local identity cues and multi-order behavioral patterns into a discriminative identity representation. Our method achieves state-of-the-art (SOTA) average performance on the SCUT-DHGA benchmark dataset, demonstrating that combining CNNs with Mamba is an effective new solution for DHGA.

</details>

Abstract: Video character replacement aims to replace actors while preserving facial expressions, motions, and scene dynamics. Existing methods typically rely on masked generation with pose guidance, limiting fine-grained expression and motion preservation in complex scenes. We propose ReXMo, an end-to-end framework that supports single-, multi-, and selective character replacement scenarios. ReXMo introduces paired triplet supervision, where synthesized driving videos and original videos share identical performances but different identities, enabling scalable training through synthetic data generation. An In-Context Diffusion Transformer learns identity transformation by jointly conditioning on driving videos, reference identities, and text prompts, while an Expression Enhancement Adapter improves facial expression preservation in multi-person scenes. Extensive experiments demonstrate that ReXMo outperforms existing methods in identity fidelity, motion consistency, expression preservation, and visual quality. Our dataset and code will be publicly released.

</details>

Abstract: Facilitative Interpersonal Skills (FIS) is a performance-based assessment method that provides a structured framework for evaluating the interpersonal competencies of mental health professionals. However, existing FIS assessment relies heavily on human observer ratings, making the evaluation process labor-intensive, costly, and difficult to scale. Progress toward automated FIS assessment has also been constrained by the lack of publicly available professional data in psychotherapy settings. To address this limitation, we introduce Therapy-FIS, a dataset for automated FIS assessment in standardized psychotherapy tasks. To our knowledge, Therapy-FIS is the first publicly available dataset in this domain. It contains facial, acoustic, and textual signals derived from standardized therapist responses to standardized mock patient stimulus clips, based on 890 therapist video recordings and 8 patient videos, and is rated with eight FIS subscale ratings and an overall FIS score. Built on Therapy-FIS, we further establish a benchmark with two downstream tasks: Therapist Performance Rating and Joint Therapist--mock-Patient Performance Rating. We also introduce a competitive baseline, DyadicFIS, with gated fusion and routed readout under therapist-only and dyadic settings. DyadicFIS obtains the best macro CCC(Concordance Correlation Coefficient) scores on both tasks (0.2807 and 0.3299). Therapy-FIS offers a reproducible resource for data-driven automated assessment of therapeutic interpersonal skills in mental health.

</details>

Abstract: Spatiotemporal scene reconstruction with Dynamic Gaussian Splatting (DGS) fundamentally depends on perfect temporal synchronization across all cameras, an assumption rarely achievable in practice. Industry-standard synchronization requires expensive specialized hardware and complex setup, while data-driven alternatives including audio-based alignment, geometry matching, and pose tracking remain limited by restrictive environmental or scene-specific assumptions. To overcome these limitations, we propose Phase-DGS, a framework that replaces conventional timestamps with a semantic phase representing each frame's inherent state within the underlying motion cycle. Frames capturing the same motion state share the same phase regardless of which camera records them or when, naturally establishing spatiotemporal correspondence across unsynchronized cameras. We validate Phase-DGS under temporal offsets, frame drops, and camera freezes, achieving up to +8.5 dB PSNR improvement over baselines while enabling seamless integration with existing DGS backbones including RealTime-4DGS and FreeTimeGS.

</details>

Abstract: We introduce BhopalMLS, a large-scale Mobile Laser Scanning (MLS) dataset from a dense, heterogeneous urban environment in Bhopal, India, with mixed traffic, informal structures, and narrow unplanned streets. It spans approximately 10 km of road corridors with over 1.2 billion points and 19,518 annotated instances across 62 fine-grained static categories, grouped into 12-class and 21-class benchmark taxonomies; 6 optional moving-class subclasses support dynamic-object analysis. Existing 3D datasets, collected in well-planned cities, are structurally more uniform than such unplanned settings, and lack their subtype diversity. On semantic and instance segmentation at both granularities, fine-tuned foundation models surpass supervised baselines: Utonia(L) reaches 71.28% mIoU (vs. PTv3's 68.04% from scratch) and 44.07% mAP50 on the 12-class benchmark. Cross-region experiments against four established datasets (Ke-MLS, Toronto-3D, Paris-Lille-3D, SemanticKITTI) reveal a consistent asymmetric transferability: models trained on BhopalMLS transfer better than the reverse on every pair, for both Utonia(L) and Concerto(L) (mean 21.5, up to 36 mIoU points), holding even when the target is denser. Factor-controlled retraining at a fixed label budget, repeated on both models, attributes this to the coverage of BhopalMLS's subtype inventory rather than to dataset size or corridor geometry: collapsing each class to a single subtype costs 13.0 and 13.3 mIoU points for Utonia(L) and Concerto(L). BhopalMLS therefore offers a testbed for 3D perception beyond well-planned cities. Data and an interactive viewer are at https://geoai4cities.github.io/bhopalmls.

</details>

Abstract: Oral photographs contain rich clinical information and hold significant potential for artificial intelligence applications; however, most models are trained for individual tasks and rely on costly expert annotations. We hypothesized that self-supervised training on unlabeled oral photographs could produce transferable visual representations for heterogeneous vision tasks. To test this hypothesis, we developed DOVE (Dental Optical Visual Encoder) by domain-adaptive self-supervised pretraining on approximately 530,000 unlabeled oral photographs collected from publicly accessible sources. DOVE was benchmarked against 3 established baselines across 5 classification datasets and 4 segmentation datasets, covering both frozen backbone evaluation and end-to-end finetuning. DOVE achieved the strongest overall performance, ranking best in few-shot transfer and label efficiency on most benchmarks. Under the 20-shot frozen-backbone protocol, DOVE outperformed the second best DINOv3 by 4.37 percentage points in mean macro-F1 across classification datasets and by 4.49 percentage points in mean macro-Dice across segmentation tasks. Under end-to-end finetuning, DOVE achieved at least 80% of the strongest full-data baseline's performance across most evaluation datasets using only 10% of the labeled training data. Qualitative retrieval and feature analyses further indicated better organization of image-level and spatial representations. These findings support DOVE as a generalizable and label-efficient backbone for oral vision research.

</details>

Abstract: Multimodal perception is central to many computer vision applications, including robotics and autonomous driving, where different sensing modalities provide complementary cues. Self-supervised learning offers a scalable solution for exploiting large amounts of unlabeled multimodal data. However, existing methods either neglect modality-specific information by enforcing feature-space alignment between modalities or learn only global representations without preserving spatial structure. We propose DenseTwins, a multimodal self-supervised framework that learns dense representations by explicitly decoupling the dense feature space into shared and modality-specific components. The proposed approach combines dense cross-modal correspondences with redundancy-reduction objectives, enabling the model to capture both shared semantics and modality-specific cues within a structured dense embedding space. DenseTwins jointly optimizes an intra-modal objective across augmented views and a cross-modal objective that aligns shared features while decorrelating modality-specific information. We evaluate DenseTwins across multiple multimodal benchmarks and sensing setups, achieving state-of-the-art or competitive performance across multimodal self-supervised baselines. Code available at: https://github.com/CarloSgaravatti/DenseTwins.

</details>


### [210] [VISAGE: Vertex-based Intrinsic Surface Appearance GEneration via Dual-Stream Diffusion for Arbitrary 3D Heads](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04712)

Abstract: Recovering identity-faithful, physically based surface appearance for 3D human heads from sparse images remains challenging. Existing methods often rely on UV-based pipelines, introducing seam artefacts, or operate in image space, limiting surface consistency. We present VISAGE, Vertex-based Intrinsic Surface Appearance GEneration, a surface-based conditional diffusion framework that synthesises per-vertex albedo and roughness directly on arbitrary mesh topologies, without requiring consistent UV parameterisation at training time. VISAGE combines global spectral processing with local geodesic K-Nearest-Neighbour refinement, enforcing structural coherence while restoring detail beyond the truncated spectral basis. Multi-view RGB images condition the network via visibility-aware cross-attention, enabling occlusion-robust synthesis across the full head surface. Region-specific modules specialise in semantically demanding and distinct areas (eyes, mouth, skin and hair), while a tangent-space output projection recovers fine iris detail. We further introduce a scalable procedural augmentation pipeline that enriches raw geometry scans with physically plausible albedo, roughness and normal maps for training. The predicted attributes render directly as a single RGBA vertex-colour channel. VISAGE reduces FID by 42.7% on NPHM and 22.5% and 4.4% on zero-shot FaceVerse and i3DMM, respectively, relative to the strongest baseline, with a 7.2M-parameter core network.

</details>


### [211] [SPOD: Recovering Suppressed Temporal Evidence in Video-LLMs via Single-Pass Orthogonal Decomposition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04728)
*Yangguang Lin,Quan Fang,Xiaoyan Liu,Zefan Xu*

Abstract: Video Large Language Models (Video-LLMs) have achieved strong performance on many video understanding tasks, yet remain vulnerable to temporal hallucination in time-sensitive reasoning scenarios such as scene transition understanding and event ordering. Existing solutions either require costly retraining or introduce inefficient multi-pass inference and external supervision at test time. In this work, we show that temporal evidence is already encoded in Video-LLM hidden representations but is not fully exploited during standard inference. Motivated by this observation, we propose Single-Pass Orthogonal Decomposition (SPOD), a training-free framework that recovers suppressed temporal evidence through analytical hidden-state manipulation in a single forward pass. SPOD consists of two components: Frame Distinctiveness Analysis (FDA), which identifies salient frames from middle-layer hidden-state geometry, and Temporal Delta Subspace (TDS), which constructs orthogonal temporal and prior subspaces from weighted inter-frame hidden-state differences. By decomposing final hidden states into temporal, prior, and residual components and rescaling them independently, SPOD enhances dynamic information, suppresses static appearance bias, and preserves complementary information. Experiments on VidHalluc, EventHallusion, and VideoMMMU show that SPOD consistently outperforms strong inference-time baselines across temporal reasoning tasks and model architectures while retaining near-vanilla inference efficiency. These results suggest that improving temporal reasoning in Video-LLMs may depend not only on additional supervision, but also on recovering temporal evidence already present in their internal representations. Our code is available at https://github.com/VioAgnes/SPOD.

</details>


### [212] [TEXMET: A Deep Learning Benchmark for Archaeological Textile Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04734)

Abstract: The reconstruction of fragmented historical textiles presents a significant challenge in computational archaeology. The Oseberg ship burial, dating to 834 CE, yielded hundreds of degraded textile fragments that have resisted systematic computational analysis. While deep learning has revolutionized image-based puzzle solving, progress on archaeological textiles has been limited by the lack of domain-specific datasets and systematic evaluation of modern architectures. To address this gap, we present TEXMET, a curated dataset of 18,644 historical textile images from the Metropolitan Museum of Art's Open Access collection, representing the first textile-specific benchmark for puzzle-solving research. Using TEXMET, we conduct a systematic evaluation of three state-of-the-art puzzle-solving models: JPDVT, FCViT, and DiffAssemble. Our experiments reveal that the transformer-based models pre-trained on standard datasets fail to generalize to textiles, but domain-specific fine-tuning proves highly effective. The graph-based DiffAssemble model, when fine-tuned on TEXMET, achieves 85.89% puzzle accuracy on textile data. Most significantly, applying this fine-tuned model to 45 highly degraded Oseberg fragments yields a performance increase from 26.67% to 64.44% puzzle accuracy. These results establish TEXMET as a new benchmark for textile reconstruction and demonstrate that domain adaptation is essential for applying puzzle-solving methods to imagery of real archaeological materials.

</details>

Abstract: Fine-grained cross-view localization aims to precisely localize a ground camera against geo-referenced aerial imagery, but current methods typically assume a narrow geographic prior, substantially limiting their applicability when upstream localization remains coarse. To bridge this gap, we study this task over much larger aerial regions. However, this seemingly simple extension fundamentally changes the localization problem: the growing disparity in spatial extent between ground and aerial views makes reliable cross-view matching difficult to establish directly, while the increasing number of look-alike locations in larger regions introduces substantial visual ambiguity. To address these challenges, we propose RingMatch, a search-and-verification framework for wide-area cross-view localization. RingMatch first leverages predictive uncertainty to uncover plausible locations and progressively zooms into their surroundings to obtain increasingly fine-grained evidence, yielding a compact set of promising candidates. We then introduce the depth ring, a novel geometric representation that reveals subtle variations in the spatial layout around a location and serves as a view-shared spatial signature for an LVLM verifier to resolve residual ambiguity. To benchmark this setting, we construct VIGOR-Pro by composing VIGOR aerial tiles into contiguous regions with spatial extents of up to 400 m. Experiments show that RingMatch maintains strong localization performance as the aerial region expands, consistently outperforming representative end-to-end and retrieval-based methods.

</details>


### [214] [Bounded AUKS: A Novel Evaluation Metric for Anomaly Detection via Bounded Area Under the Kolmogorov--Smirnov Curve](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04754)
*Yanshuo Zhang,Juan Du*

Main category: cs.CV

Method: 解析错误

Result: 解析错误

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: Recent literature widely used global metrics such as AUROC and AUPRO to quantify the detection performance. AUROC and AUPRO scores are approaching 100% in some public datasets such as MVTec AD dataset and VisA dataset, which misleadingly suggests that the pixel-level anomaly detection task has already been solved. Threshold-independent metrics represented by AUROC inherently ignore the threshold selection process, which is inevitable in practical applications. Although AUPRO restricts the False Positive rates (FPRs) to an acceptable range, extremely low FPRs can lead to increased false negative rates (FNRs). We argue that there is a lack of metrics capable of quantifying the separation between the anomaly score distributions of normal and anomalous samples, which hinders advancements in anomaly detection. In response, we propose the Bounded Area Under the Kolmogorov--Smirnov curve (Bounded AUKS), a partial integral metric that integrates the difference between TPR and FPR within a data-driven bounded range. Specifically, we introduce AUKS from KS integral as a direct measure of separability between the anomaly score distributions. The bounded range is determined only by score distributions of normal test samples. Lower bound excludes the uninformative high-FPR region. Upper bound avoids evaluation in extreme regions that aggressively push FPR towards zero, which result in a very low recall. Our experiments on 9 models across MVTec AD and VisA datasets show that Bounded AUKS can address the limitations of existing metrics and has a better performance in evaluating unsupervised anomaly detection models.

</details>

Abstract: We present minimal solvers for joint homography and radial distortion estimation using affine- or orientation-covariant features. The core contribution is a set of novel constraints that exploit local feature geometry inherent to affine descriptors, enabling solvers that require fewer correspondences than point-based methods. Using modern robust estimation frameworks, we demonstrate that the proposed solvers are advantageous to the current state-of-the-art with a significant speed-up in the presence of outliers while maintaining comparable or superior estimation accuracy. The proposed solvers are tested on both synthetic and real images containing challenging fisheye distortions.

</details>


### [216] [Pixel-space video generation with U-JiT](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04764)
