Abstract: Imitation learning policies in robotics are trained on datasets collected through costly real-world teleoperation. Often, the data-collection process is unguided, relying on the teleoperator's intuition, resulting in data that contributes little to overall policy performance. Recent work has shown that curating such unguided datasets can substantially improve policy performance, but these approaches operate by filtering existing datasets after collection and therefore do not prevent the acquisition of uninformative data. In this work, we propose GUILD, a novel Guided Data Collection (GDC) method that actively reasons about which data should be collected to maximize downstream policy performance. We introduce an iterative framework that alternates between 1) task proposal and data collection and 2) data evaluation to close the loop. For task proposal, we leverage semantic priors from vision-language models to identify relevant environments, object configurations, and goals for data collection. However, since semantic relevance does not imply policy improvement, we perform data evaluation. By leveraging predictive data attribution we approximate how the collected trajectories influence policy performance on a target deployment setting. This signal is then used as feedback to guide subsequent data collection, closing the data collection loop. We evaluate our approach in simulation and real robot settings, demonstrating that guided data collection substantially improves sample efficiency and deployment performance compared to passive data collection strategies, multiple baselines and experienced roboticists. More information at https://robin-lab.cs.utexas.edu/guild2026/

</details>

Abstract: Inverse kinematics (IK) converts end-effector pose commands into joint motion, and many vision-language-action (VLA) policies rely on it to act on a given arm and to transfer across embodiments. Precise IK remains laborious: redundancy and singularities complicate the inverse mapping, execution depends on hand-tuned controllers, and training data on physical robots are costly to collect. We present PolicyIK, a goal-conditioned reinforcement learning policy for high-precision IK. It updates joint-position references from pose feedback and observes its accumulated reference relative to the measured joints, so it can retain the offset that the position controller needs at rest. Two-scale pose errors with an error-dependent action gain resolve millimeter residuals while preserving large motions. Progress and dwell rewards teach it to both reach the tolerance and stay within it. In simulation, a single attempt by the trained PPO policy keeps the mean errors over the final second within 1 mm and 0.2° on 93.5% of held-out tasks, and a deployment protocol raises success to 98.9%. When trained directly on a physical arm with an off-policy RL learner, PolicyIK achieves higher reaching and holding success than all four classical IK controllers under the same time budgets. In simulated pick-and-place, PolicyIK executes the end-effector commands of the $π_{0.5}$ VLA policy with 95.7% success. These results show that reinforcement learning can provide high-precision inverse kinematics for robot arms. Code is available at https://github.com/Tinaliu0123/PolicyIK.

</details>

Abstract: Millimeter-wave (mmWave) links can lose tens of decibels within milliseconds when a person or vehicle crosses the line of sight, and reactive handover recovers only after the outage has begun. Integrated sensing and communication (ISAC) promises to let the network see blockers coming and move users to another cell in advance. This paper asks how much such foresight is worth relative to the best reactive handover, and what sensing accuracy is needed to harvest it. We formulate cost-aware cell selection with knowledge of the future signal-to- noise ratio as a sequential decision problem, define the value of foresight through a policy-independent bound, and develop an ISAC-assisted handover framework in which a radio unit senses blockers with its own communication waveform and a simulated Open RAN xApp plans handovers. Ray-traced experiments in a 28 GHz street canyon, reported on held-out scenarios, show that, with a short look-ahead, perfect foresight closes most of the gap between service-aware (Layer-3) reactive handover and an instantaneous oracle. Harvesting this value, however, requires the user and nearby blockers to be localized to about a decimeter, well beyond what our radar tracker and meter- level user positioning provide; with realistic errors, even an uncertainty-aware planner largely defers to reactive handover.

</details>


### [370] [Adaptive Low-Rank Quantum Kernel Acquisition under Finite-Shot Measurements: Perturbation Guarantees for QSVMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03777)
*Duong The Do,Duong Tung Nguyen*

Main category: eess.SY

TL;DR: 解析错误


<details>

Motivation: 解析错误

Method: 解析错误

Result: 解析错误

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: Quantum kernel methods require estimating entries of a dense Gram matrix through repeated circuit measurements, making kernel construction expensive and statistically noisy. For a dataset of size $n$, dense construction requires $O(n^2)$ kernel queries, while existing approximation methods typically assume inexpensive access to kernel entries or use non-adaptive sampling. We study adaptive low-rank quantum kernel acquisition when the kernel is accessible only through a finite-shot quantum overlap oracle. Our framework, ICAQKL, uses residual-driven incomplete Cholesky construction to acquire informative kernel columns sequentially and form a positive-semidefinite rank-$r$ surrogate, reducing the training-kernel acquisition cost to $O(nrN_s)$ circuit executions for $r\ll n$, where $N_s$ is the number of shots per queried entry. The central difficulty is that finite-shot errors perturb both the queried kernel values and the adaptive pivot sequence that determines the low-rank factor. We develop a non-asymptotic perturbation framework for this stochastic adaptive process, establishing concentration over the realized queries, stability of noisy pivot selection and factor construction, and propagation of the coupled sampling and approximation errors to the QSVM objective, optimizer, prediction function, and classification margin. The resulting rank--shot conditions characterize when ICAQKL approaches the full QSVM induced by the same quantum feature map. Experiments on benchmark and biomedical datasets show favorable accuracy--query-cost tradeoffs, competitive performance with the full QSVM, and competitive or improved performance relative to the evaluated Nyström baselines under comparable settings. ICAQKL is intended to enable measurement-efficient and theoretically controlled use of a selected quantum kernel, rather than to establish quantum advantage over classical kernels.

</details>

Abstract: Advanced attitude determination and control (ADCS) techniques can be difficult for small satellite teams to adopt: tightly coupled implementations require substantial code changes to evaluate different actuators, sensors, orbits, mission goals, and control laws. Existing tools often emphasize orbit mechanics or flight software rather than closed-loop ADCS. This paper presents an open-source, modular ADCS framework in pure Python for rapid development, repeatable testing, and flight-oriented integration across heterogeneous spacecraft.
  The package supports torque- and command-level control, bound-respecting allocation, and full- and reduced-attitude objectives. Interchangeable estimation, control, planning, and actuator/sensor modules are reconfigured through configuration changes. Integrated simulation includes attitude and orbit propagation, sensor and actuator bias and noise, and environmental disturbances. An actuator-aware planner supports underactuated architectures, while hardware-in-the-loop (HIL) capability enables testing on mission-representative computers.
  Case studies include a 3U CubeSat with three magnetorquers and one reaction wheel, a magnetorquer-only 1U, and a 6U with three reaction wheels and thrusters. These demonstrate actuator and sensor failures, full- and reduced-attitude goals, and momentum management. HIL testing on a Raspberry Pi uses the same package. Adapting a published control law to a new actuator configuration requires fewer than five lines of configuration changes. The framework underpins flight software for an Earth-observing CubeSat with visible and long-wave infrared imagers, three magnetorquers, and one reaction wheel, without star trackers or propulsion.

</details>

Abstract: The Musical Metaverse (MM) enables geographically distributed musicians, audiences, sound engineers, and AI agents to interact through synchronized audio, visual, kinematic, and haptic streams, imposing latency, synchronization, bandwidth, and scalability requirements that are difficult to satisfy with conventional bit-level communication. This paper argues that semantic communication, understood here as the transmission of musically meaningful information rather than raw audio-visual-haptic samples, provides a principled communication paradigm for the MM. We define the MM from a communication perspective and derive modality-specific require ments; propose a three-pillar framework for musical semantic communication comprising semantics extraction, semantic-aware orchestration, and cross-modal semantic synchronization; survey representative work across semantic communication, music AI and edge orchestration, identifying the gaps that prevent their integration; and propose a semantic-first reference architecture based on device-side semantic encoders, edge semantic routers, and cross-modal semantic synchronization. The resulting framework positions the MM as a concrete and demanding use case for 6G semantic communication.

</details>

Abstract: Max-Pressure (MP) has emerged as a widely studied decentralized traffic signal control framework, thanks to its desirable theoretical properties and strong control performance. Early research on MP focused on improving control performance by exploring different variables of the traffic state for the definition of pressure. Subsequent studies expanded MP to incorporate the complexity of real-world transportation systems and emerging technologies. This paper provides a comprehensive review of these developments. The review first introduces the theoretical foundations of MP and examines early developments in movement weight definition. It then synthesizes major extensions for multimodal and multiscale network control, followed by recent developments in connected and automated vehicle (CAV) environments and the integration of MP with artificial intelligence (AI) and data-driven methods. Beyond summarizing existing approaches, this review examines their underlying assumptions, data requirements, implementation considerations, and practical limitations. Based on this comprehensive review, key research gaps and future directions are identified, including remaining limitations in the theoretical foundations of MP and the underexplored integration of autonomous vehicle control with the MP framework. Overall, this review provides a structured perspective on the evolution and current state of MP control and highlights opportunities for developing more proactive, robust, and practically deployable MP-based traffic control methods.

</details>

Abstract: Capacity expansion models often dramatically simplify the physics of how power flows through a transmission network in order to preserve computational tractability. However, with the rapid growth of electrification, large loads, renewables, and storage, increased realism in planning future grids is imperative. We extend the open-source capacity expansion model GenX with AC optimal power flow physics, and analyze generation, storage, and transmission investment decisions for 20-zone, hourly models of Germany and Texas. We compare our results against those using two common approximations, the transport model and DC optimal power flow. We find that total costs and emissions largely agree across all formulations. However, we find substantial differences in outputs related to transmission, generation siting, storage, and reactive-power support between the approximate formulations and the true AC physics formulation. We identify where AC physics changes planning decisions and discuss how to potentially capture these effects without the full computational cost.

</details>

Abstract: The letter aims to improve the math-to-real or math-to-sim transfer of a mission controller designed for a Temporal Reach Avoid Stay (T-RAS) specification. Our approach uses the control-theoretic concept of Incremental Input-to-State stability ($δ$-ISS). We model the real-world system as an uncertain system, represented by a nominal mathematical model with an additive disturbance. Our method involves two steps: firstly, we make the nominal mathematical model and the real-world system $δ$-ISS. Secondly, we design a mission controller for the mathematical model that satisfies a modified specification. We then prove that applying the same mission-level input sequence to the real-world system guarantees that the original specification is satisfied in the real world. Videos of real-world experiments are available at https://youtu.be/NvjkIZxtnPM.

Abstract: This paper considers online intrusion-risk assessment and graded warning for unmanned aerial vehicles under bounded measurement attacks. To address this problem, we develop a monitoring framework that combines trusted state estimation, attack-aware risk assessment, and probabilistic signal temporal logic (PrSTL). Trusted observations support recursive state estimation, while potentially attacked observations inform integrity testing and intrusion-risk assessment. The key contribution is a confidence-set construction that combines normalized innovation statistics with physical attack bounds to characterize admissible measurement biases. The resulting family of bias-corrected Gaussian posteriors yields an upper bound on the probability of occupying a no-fly zone or its surrounding safety margin. Confidence guarantees and finite-cover numerical certificates quantify the reliability of this risk envelope, which is evaluated with or without an integrity alarm. The PrSTL monitor analyzes the resulting risk sequence to distinguish ordered, persistent, and repeated risk and produces graded warnings with explicit robustness margins and supporting windows. We validate the monitor in flight experiments with a Crazyflie quadrotor tracked by a Vicon motion capture system. Controlled comparisons quantify the contributions of individual monitoring components. Prescribed risk profiles verify the temporal semantics and illustrate how distinct risk patterns produce different warning levels.

</details>

Abstract: Funnels, time-varying invariant sets around nominal trajectories, certify that a closed-loop system tracks its plan, and can be composed into verified motion plans. Their computation by sum-of-squares (SOS) programming is well established in formulation but not in practice: the result depends on implementation choices such as the initial guess, Lyapunov function normalization, and termination rule, which the literature documents only briefly. This paper presents a self-contained treatment of SOS-based funnel computation, implemented in the open-source package computeSOSFunnels. We propose a minimum-volume formulation with inlet and outlet constraints under which funnels with coinciding nominal endpoints compose by construction. Numerical experiments on a unicycle, with timing studies on a cart-pole and a 12-state quadrotor, characterize the pipeline: infeasible initial guesses are partly decidable before solving, the certificate shows no violations among 39,000 boundary samples per funnel under the true dynamics, and constructing the semidefinite program, not solving it, dominates computation time.

</details>

Abstract: Can market notices read by a large language model (LLM) improve short-horizon electricity price spike forecasts? We study five regions of Australia's National Electricity Market. A local LLM extracted features once per notice, only notices visible at the forecast origin were used, and a gradient-boosting model produced onset probabilities. In an out-of-sample development back-test (July 2022 to July 2024), notice features did not lower the 60-minute Brier score of a numeric model including the market operator's next-hour forecasts. At 24 hours they did, but rule-based features did so almost equally and attribution to notice content was not established. A locally frozen protocol, not publicly deposited, governed a retrospective single-use holdout from 15 August to 17 October 2026. At 60 minutes, the hypothesis that LLM features lower the numeric model's Brier score was not established (H1; relative Brier skill -0.09%), so the planned comparison with rule-based features was not tested (H2). At 24 hours, the hypothesis that LLM features lower the score against a text-free control was not established (H3; +0.37%, 95% CI [-9.74, 1.39], inconclusive). By the frozen wording rule the non-significant 24-hour improvement comes mainly from recalibration and cannot be attributed to notice content, although post hoc the decomposition could not separate recalibration from resolution. The 24-hour base rate was 1.56% on the holdout against 14.0% in development. With 5 onsets on 3 days and 24-hour positives in 2 of 22 three-day blocks, the holdout neither established nor excluded a 24-hour effect.

</details>

Abstract: Wheel-legged robots balance on two wheels while using articulated legs to change height, absorb impacts, and cross uneven terrain. On hardware, the balance controller must rely on noisy inertial and encoder measurements, and it must reject disturbances that are not known in advance, such as payloads and wind, without exceeding motor torque limits. This paper presents a moving horizon estimator (MHE) combined with an offset-free, constrained model predictive controller (MPC) for the sagittal balance of a 9.95 kg wheel-legged robot. The estimator uses a virtual-leg model augmented with a horizontal hip force, a body pitch torque, and a gyroscope bias. Its accelerometer model removes the predicted hip acceleration, and its process noise is increased when an impact is detected. We evaluate the approach in MuJoCo with simulated sensor noise, bias, quantization, and delay, and compare it with LQR baselines that use ground-truth state, a complementary filter, or the same MHE. In a driving, squatting, reversing, and bowing sequence, MPC with MHE reduced the RMS speed error to 0.11 m/s, compared with 0.24 m/s for LQR with ground-truth state. Under an unknown 2 kg payload and a 10 N headwind, the mean position error in wind was 6 cm, compared with 81 cm for LQR with ground-truth state. Across a 3 $\times$ 4 grid of payloads and headwinds, LQR with the same estimator fell in 3 of 12 combinations, compared with 1 of 12 for MPC. These results are limited to simulation on flat ground, and experimental validation on hardware remains future work.

</details>

Abstract: We propose a Multi-technology AdopTion-opinion co-Evolution (MATE) model to describe the diffusion of $m \geq 2$ competing alternatives within a networked population whose opinions evolve under social influence and the feedback generated by adoption. Communities interact through two layers: a physical network governing exposure to adoption, and a social network shaping opinion evolution through social imitation. Individuals can adopt a technology, eventually becoming dissatisfied at a rate reflecting the quality of the technology, and switch to an alternative at a rate driven by their opinion of that alternative. For an arbitrary number of competing technologies $m \geq 2$, we characterize the equilibria and establish the existence of an adoption-diffused equilibrium in which all technologies coexist. For $m > 2$, the equilibrium configuration depends on the broader competitive environment: unlike the two-technology case, within-community relative adoption generally depends also on equilibrium opinions and on the other alternatives. Finally, we formulate finite-horizon opinion-shaping control problems, derive necessary optimality conditions via the discrete-time Pontryagin maximum principle, and compute numerical extremals using a forward-backward sweep method.

</details>

Abstract: The energy transition causes a growing number of distributed flexible energy resources in the low voltage grid. To utilize that flexibility, automation and control algorithms are needed, which typically are developed in theoretical research. We build a cyberphysical algorithm test bed to bridge the gap between theoretical research and practical application in cooperation with a local DSO in Walenstadt, Switzerland. To obtain suitable monitoring and data analysis capabilities from their existing data landscape, we propose tool-independent data harmonization measures in preparation for a digital twin of the grid. For data extraction from high-security network zones we developed a secure pipeline that implements a logical data diode ensuring one-way traffic only. Furthermore, we established a test community of voluntary prosumer households, whose flexible assets were made accessible through a common backend, giving us actuation capabilities. The presented publish-subscribe architecture is not only suitable to integrate various data sources into a central data collection, but also to manage access privileges of different stakeholders. This could support unbundling of an organization's data flows and the implementation of data space concepts.

Abstract: The growing autonomy of AI agents has enabled self-improvement, where agents iteratively refine their programs through execution feedback and performance evaluation. As these agents increasingly operate in physical environments and rely on wireless connectivity, communication conditions become an integral part of their improvement process. However, existing approaches largely assume ideal communication, overlooking how wireless conditions shape both the feedback that guides program modifications and the evaluation outcomes that determine which modifications survive. This challenge is further complicated by the coupling between agent behavior and wireless connectivity, as agents influence their own communication conditions through mobility and transmission decisions. To address this problem, we propose WiSI, a wireless-in-the-loop self-improvement framework that integrates spatiotemporal wireless channels, edge-assisted perception, and Large Language Model-driven program improvement. By incorporating wireless execution into the improvement loop, WiSI reveals how wireless execution experience shapes the generation and retention of program structures, and investigates the wireless conditions needed for robust improvement. Experiments show that agents improved under ideal links achieve only 40.0% task success under congestion, compared with 77.2% when improved under realistic wireless conditions. More importantly, wireless-aware feedback and selection jointly enable agents to discover and retain communication-efficient program structures that outperform parameter tuning and remain robust even with inaccurate run-time channel observations. We further find that moderate-load or mixed-network evaluation maintains strong performance across deployment conditions, while simplified models preserving link-rate statistics achieve comparable results without detailed spatial channel information.

</details>

Abstract: Residual deployment can affect closed-loop tracking. We compare two ways to deploy one supervised acceleration-residual network in nonlinear model predictive control (NMPC) of a quadrotor. Cyclewise deployment repeats one correction across the horizon. Stagewise deployment evaluates shifted prediction nodes before the solve. Network parameters remain fixed during control. On prescribed force pulses, both schemes improve average tracking mainly after force removal, but worsen hover; some prediction checks also fail. Seed 1 reinforcement-learning benchmarks add no mean benefit under pulses or prescribed random forces. On 220 conditions, cyclewise and stagewise supervision reduce mean integral absolute position error (IAE) by 4.38% and 7.77%. On a separate 32-condition bank, fixed gain 1.5 lowers IAE by 27.47% relative to stagewise supervision but raises angular-rate root-mean-square error by 10.19%. In one synthetic computational fluid dynamics field, stagewise supervision lowers four-task mean IAE by 2.01% against a same-graph zero-residual control; some secondary metrics worsen. These simulations show that residual deployment affects tracking, with gains and limitations that depend on task and motion metric.

</details>

Abstract: Integral quadratic constraints (IQCs) and scaled relative graphs (SRGs) are two prominent frameworks for analyzing feedback systems. Although both frameworks are rooted in the same graph separation principle, a general characterization of their relationship has remained unavailable. This paper establishes conditions under which the IQC- and SRG frameworks are equivalent. To this end, we introduce two new classes of systems, termed disjunctive and conjunctive systems, which are respectively characterized by unions and intersections of quadratic input-output constraints. Under structural assumptions, we prove equivalence between the IQC and SRG frameworks for these system classes. By exploiting this equivalence, we develop novel IQC- and SRG-based converse stability results for linear and nonlinear systems. Our results accommodate multiple quadratic constraints, extending well-known converse results in robust control beyond the single-constraint setting.

</details>

Abstract: Large Language Model (LLM)-based agents can automate complex engineering workflows by interacting with tools, interpreting feedback, and making iterative decisions. This paper introduces TuneBench, a benchmark for evaluating Agentic AI in power system dynamic model calibration, where controller parameters are adjusted to reproduce target dynamic responses. The benchmark reflects a common Transmission System Operator (TSO) practice, in which equivalent models used for Dynamic Security Assessment (DSA) are calibrated against measurements from grid-code compliance tests when proprietary models are unavailable. Two cases of increasing complexity are considered: the governor and Automatic Voltage Regulator (AVR) of a synchronous generator and the control system of a Battery Energy Storage System (BESS), both tuned against active-power and voltage step tests within a budget of 15 simulations. LLM-based agents are compared with random search and Genetic Algorithm (GA) baselines over 10 independent runs. Only the most advanced models, Opus 5.5 and GPT-6, accurately reproduce all target trajectories, with median R2 above 0.995 in every test and tracking errors up to two orders of magnitude lower than the baselines. The agent logs show that these agents autonomously extract dynamic performance indices, such as settling time and damping, and relate them to specific control parameters, decomposing the tuning problem into physically interpretable sub-problems. The benchmark is publicly available at https://github.com/Power-Agent/PowerAgentBench/tree/main/benchmarks/dynamic/level3.

</details>

Abstract: The effect of Tai Chi postures on the mechanical response of the body to external pushes is usually assessed with clinical balance scores, which say little about the underlying dynamics. We identify, for five subjects and eight postural conditions, linear models from the mechanical work transferred by a pendulum hitting the forearm to the change of the antero-posterior ground reaction force. Each trial contains 10 to 16 impacts, and each impact yields a transient of 0.55\,s only. Building on earlier analyses of these data, we improve the processing chain in three ways. The input is related to the energy lost by the pendulum and its repeatability is quantified. The output is defined component-wise, which makes it independent of the zeroing of the force plate. Finally, the impacts are stacked in a multi-experiment least-squares problem and validated on unseen impacts. Tenth-order ARX models then reach a median leave-one-impact-out fit of 65\%, against 72\% for the repeatability of the response itself, and never require stability enforcement, unlike estimation on averaged impacts. Closing the eyes lengthens the identified delay by about 70\,ms in all subjects, and the Tai Chi posture increases the antero-posterior force transmitted per joule in four of five subjects. Surface EMG envelopes show a tonic co-activation of the lower-leg muscles in this posture, a smaller reactive triceps burst and a fast habituation of the tibialis response. Together, they point to a stiffer, anticipatory postural strategy.

</details>

Abstract: Embodied intelligent applications have expanded from air and ground into maritime environments, where missions such as coastal reporting require coordination among underwater sensing, surface gateways, and aerial relays. Yet field trials remain costly, and existing simulation tools make it difficult to study how cross-medium communication affects cooperation among these platforms. We present TriMedium, an integrated Air-Ground-Sea simulator for cross-medium embodied networking. TriMedium has three design elements. First, it provides a shared three-dimensional world where aerial, terrestrial, and underwater entities coexist, so underwater agent motion and visual observations stay geometrically consistent with air and ground nodes. Second, it uses a common simulation clock that preserves observation timestamps while scheduling delayed communication and computation, clarifying when the shore receiver obtains each observation. Third, modular quantization and compression interfaces accommodate heterogeneous telemetry and image pipelines, while integrity checks separate intentional lossy distortion from incomplete or corrupted delivery. Three case studies validate TriMedium. Multimodal data delivery tests telemetry and image pipelines, scene construction examines cross-domain spatial consistency, and positioning assesses geometric accuracy under delayed communication. Together, they show TriMedium enables embodied networking by quantifying cross-medium delivery dynamics and fidelity across heterogeneous payloads.

</details>

Abstract: Mean-shift exploration enables assignment-free swarm shape assembly, but obstacle-constrained navigation additionally requires coordinated adaptation of the desired morphology and its physical execution. This paper presents an environment-adaptive navigation framework that integrates target-field planning, execution regulation, and morphology-preserving mean-shift exploration. A feasibility-aware graph search plans the position, orientation, and morphology of an unlabeled target field without specifying robot-specific goals. An execution-regulated reference manager adjusts field progression and morphology switching using swarm entering, coverage, and lag metrics. A moving-frame controller separates prescribed rigid motion from local mean-shift redistribution and incorporates neighbor interactions and obstacle responses. Simulations and hardware experiments demonstrate that the proposed framework coordinates morphology transitions and collective motion for assignment-free swarm navigation through cluttered environments.

</details>

Abstract: We study finite-horizon safety verification for continuous-time stochastic systems modeled by stochastic differential equations. Given an open and bounded safe set, a compact initial set, a finite time horizon, and a prescribed violation-probability threshold, the objective is to certify that the exit probability from the safe set before the horizon remains below the threshold. Under uniform ellipticity of the diffusion matrix and regularity of the safe-set boundary, we develop a time-dependent barrier-like condition expressed as a set of functional inequalities, obtained by relaxing a parabolic initial-boundary value problem associated with the exit probability. If a function satisfies the condition, then the probability of leaving the safe set before the horizon is guaranteed to remain below the threshold. Conversely, when the exit probability is strictly smaller than the prescribed threshold uniformly over the initial set, the barrier-like condition is also necessary: there exists a smooth function satisfying it. Furthermore, when the dynamics are polynomial and the set descriptions are semialgebraic, the problem of verifying the condition admits a sum-of-squares formulation that is both sound and complete, yielding a complete degree hierarchy for finite-horizon stochastic safety verification.

</details>

These assumptions have yet to be formally justified. We present concise mathematical proofs validating the claim for certain metrics (expected unserved energy and effective load carrying capability), but also provide simple invalidating counterexamples for others (loss-of-load expectation and equivalent firm capacity). Formulations built on non-convex (non-concave) metrics structurally undervalue candidate portfolio adequacy, and so cannot claim to produce least-cost expansion plans.

Abstract: This letter addresses the stability analysis of discrete-time linear systems with time-varying delay using a Lyapunov-Krasovskii functional (LKF) framework. An improved matrix-separation-based inequality (IMSBI) is developed to obtain tighter bounds for the summation terms arising in the forward difference of the LKF. Based on this result, an augmented LKF incorporating additional cross-terms is constructed to capture the interaction between system states and delay evolution. This leads to a less conservative delay-dependent stability criterion expressed as linear matrix inequalities (LMIs). Numerical results demonstrate improved stability margins and a reduced number of decision variables compared with existing methods.

</details>

Abstract: Proactive intersection safety analysis depends on trajectory data whose reliability varies by sensing modality, yet infrastructure-perception and surrogate safety measure (SSM) research have only recently begun to converge. This paper synthesizes both literatures alongside control theory to clarify how sensing choices, safety metrics, and predictive control connect end-to-end, and where infrastructure-derived risk signals could inform autonomous vehicle decision-making. Searches covered light detection and ranging (LiDAR), cooperative perception, time-to-collision (TTC), post-encroachment time (PET), extreme value theory, model predictive control (MPC), and signal control. Screening considered trajectory-based safety and control relevance, excluding detection benchmarks without safety linkage. This structured narrative synthesis retained 56 sources. No single SSM is universally valid: one-dimensional TTC and modified TTC suit longitudinal conflicts, two-dimensional TTC extends time-based measurement to crossing geometries, and observed PET captures crossing and vulnerable road user (VRU) conflicts but only after the event. LiDAR consistently outperforms camera-only sensing for continuous three-dimensional trajectory extraction, illumination robustness, and privacy, though its range degrades in heavy rain, fog, and snow. Its output becomes control-ready only when paired with filtering--Kalman, Savitzky-Golay--that stabilizes noisy detections. This review complements it from the infrastructure side: it examines how roadside sensing affects that chain's reliability, classifies SSMs by conflict geometry and temporal availability, and maps risk components onto control objectives and constraints for infrastructure and connected and automated vehicle (CAV) applications.

</details>

Learning such models from data requires a choice of representation and fitting method. We compare using neural networks, analytic expressions and combinations of the two for representing the different components of the port-Hamiltonian system, and compare fitting the models with gradient-based optimisation or sparse regression. Abstract: Online dynamic security assessment and day-ahead planning require rapid identification of where disturbances will produce significant dynamic activity. Full time-domain simulations are computationally demanding, while existing machine-learning approaches predict system-level outcomes or require post-disturbance trajectories. This paper proposes a pair-aware Graph Isomorphism Network with Edge features (GINE) framework comprising two task-specific models for predicting bus-voltage and generator apparent-power activity from the operating point and contingency description. Physically aware KPI cuts convert simulated responses into ordinal activity targets for model training. Evaluations on Nordic 32 and the French transmission system with more than 6 000 buses show that pair-aware fusion and graph embeddings improve on a local baseline on Nordic 32, with GINE the lower-cost graph encoder, and that the framework remains effective at large scale. As a downstream application, the predicted activity maps guide Adaptive Model Simplification, reducing continuous and discrete model variables by 72% and 66%, achieving speedups of up to 3.16x, and preserving voltage trajectories when critical substations are retained. These results demonstrate scalable, pre-disturbance prediction of component-level dynamic activity.

</details>

Abstract: Accurately predicting eddy-current and hysteresis losses in mild steel components such as the structural parts and tank of power transformers remains a challenge in transformer design. Conventional numerical approaches for eddy-current loss analysis require discretization at a fraction of the skin depth. While accurate, such fine volumetric mesh becomes computationally prohibitive for large power transformers. Moreover, hysteresis loss is not always included in this analysis. To investigate the trade-off between computational cost and accuracy, this work presents a comparative analysis of eight distinct modeling frameworks for predicting both eddy-current and hysteresis losses in mild steel. These include efficient methods avoiding meshing, such as standard impedance boundary conditions, and more accurate approaches retaining full volumetric meshing, namely complex permeability, nonlinear single-valued B-H curve, and Jiles-Atherton hysteresis modeling. To accurately represent the material's electromagnetic behavior, the properties of the mild steel used are measured directly from samples. Finally, loss measurements are conducted to benchmark all eight frameworks, demonstrating mean absolute percentage errors between predictions and measurements generally below 20%.

</details>

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) lack physical grounding and extrapolation. This paper formalises the Novelty Horizon as tasks needing extrapolation beyond training support, causal reasoning absent from learned correlations, or post-frontier information. A forensic analysis tests LLMs on unguided, guided, and ground-truth data from an underrepresented diode-clamped short-circuit experiment using temporal, spectral, and statistical validation against a first-principles Modified Ohm's Law. Both script-based approaches fail catastrophically with physical and spectral inconsistencies, extreme statistical pathologies, zero Generative Fidelity Score, and linear-regime outputs. Direct prompting corroborates distinct failure modes. This paper introduces the Past Information Boundary and physics-based authentication. LLM data generation cannot extrapolate past the Novelty Horizon essential for novel discovery. Hybrid neuro-symbolic systems must preserve human causal reasoning and experimental design.

</details>

Abstract: OperAItor, an open-source framework for developing and evaluating large language model agents for supervisory fault management in chemical processes. The framework is demonstrated on a dynamic reactor-distillation process representing isopropanol dehydrogenation, with physically based thermodynamics and modified reaction kinetics to permit liquid-phase operation. The process is represented by a 113-variable differential-algebraic model and operates at a nominal feed rate of 100kmol/h, producing approximately 4t/h of acetone. The simulated plant includes 8 PI control loops, 22 process alarms, 5 duty-standby pump trains, and 11 independent safety instrumented functions. The agent architecture integrates multivariate statistical process monitoring, fault diagnosis, knowledge-graph retrieval, action planning, and supervisory action generation. Five action-validation configurations are investigated, comprising operation without validation, an LLM-based judge, digital-twin simulation, and deterministic admissibility checks with and without consequence projection. The framework is evaluated using four LLMs across 20 scenarios involving single and multiple simultaneous faults. Without agent intervention, 18 scenarios result in plant stops, corresponding to an economic loss of $399,000 relative to fault-free operation under the assumed benchmark economics. For GPT-5 nano, Qwen3 8B, and Mistral 7B, validated agent configurations reduce the number of stops to 5-8 and recover $265,000 of the loss. The study provides a reproducible benchmark for comparing agent architectures, language models, and action-validation methods for supervisory fault management in chemical processes.

</details>

Abstract: A general mathematical model of experience formation is considered, in which the learning level is defined as the probability that the next value of the uncertainty factor has already been mastered and not forgotten. Within this model the shape of the learning curve is derived, and it turns out to be determined primarily by the distribution of situation occurrence: a uniform distribution yields an exponential curve, whereas a power-law distribution yields a power-law curve whose exponent equals the ratio of the distribution exponent to that same exponent increased by one. Both mechanisms generating power-law curves, established earlier in the analysis of debugging, assembly and repair activities, are shown to have been independently rediscovered in deep learning theory as the "frequency" and "spectral" mechanisms of scaling laws for artificial neural networks, while the exponential curves of machine learning (contraction operators, strongly convex optimization, stationary reinforcement) are generated by the same constancy of the contraction coefficient that was revealed half a century earlier in models of radioactive decay, the first-order lag element, and others. A solution is given for the problem of choosing a training distribution that differs from the deployment distribution. A methodological consequence is discussed: three distinct mechanisms generate curves indistinguishable in shape, so an observed learning curve does not allow one to judge the "internal" structure of the learning system.

</details>

Abstract: This paper investigates iterative methods for solving feedback-type linear equations under small phase condition. We first develop an iterative solution scheme for linear equations with sectorial (semi-sectorial) matrices, i.e., matrices whose numerical ranges are contained in an open (closed) half complex plane. Exploiting the geometric properties of the numerical range, we introduce the notion of numerical-radius contraction as a convergence criterion. Such a scheme, with some extra effort, leads to an iterative method for the feedback-type equations under small phase condition, whose convergence condition is characterized in terms of phase information. This complements classical iterative methods for feedback-type equations based on the small gain condition. A case study on large-scale power networks is presented to illustrate the computational efficiency of the proposed method.

</details>

Abstract: Continuous video scales in cost with camera count, resolution and recording duration, yet many monitoring tasks seek events rather than complete images and can operate on a compact single-pixel measurement stream. Recovering a chosen interval as video is then desirable, but at 0.24\% of the pixel count the scene correlation image looks like noise and per-clip optimization takes minutes. Correlating the residual of an estimate with the coding patterns nevertheless gives a residual image, the gradient of a centered measurement loss. Lifted through a pretrained video decoder, its agreement with the true error exceeds that of control decoders. Video-latent inversion (VLI) then outperforms frame-wise image-latent reconstruction at 1{,}000 bucket values. Measurement-Gradient-Conditioned Video Flow (MGV-Flow), trained once to read the lifted residual image with preceding frames, reconstructs five frames in 1.55\,s at 22.9\,dB median PSNR, against 398\,s and 18.1\,dB for VLI, with the largest gains in high-motion scenes. Directional fidelity ranks 30 coding configurations and selects one needing a third fewer bucket values. On a physical system, MGV-Flow outperforms per-frame reconstruction without retraining. A sliding window over the bucket-value stream yields 3.2 frames per second on one RTX~4090 and, in theory, 20 frames per second on a data-centre accelerator. Demonstrations are available at https://cavedweller509.github.io/MGV-Flow.

</details>

Jacobian determinants showed no folding on the sampling grid. Group-level linear mixed-effects maps of change from baseline (AFNI 3dLME), computed identically under both pipelines, differ in the size, number and location of their suprathreshold clusters. In this dataset, the registration pipeline is therefore a variable that can change the interpretation of downstream group-level analyses.

Abstract: State-of-the-art bronchoscopy guidance systems and computer-aided diagnosis methods have emerged as important tools for early lung cancer detection. Effective use of these tools depends on being able to accurately link a patient's bronchoscopic airway exam video to their pre-operative 3D chest CT scan. Unfortunately, the derivation of such links, which include bronchoscope depth and camera motion, from the exam video remains an open problem. Building on recently proposed 3D-vision foundation models, we propose DUNEDepth, a hybrid architecture that combines a shared DUNE ViT encoder, a twin MASt3R cross-attention decoder structure, and a prediction head that includes elements of the MASt3R and Reloc3r models. To fine-tune the architecture to our application, we utilize an integrated hybrid synthetic-supervised/self-supervised training strategy that draws on both bronchoscopic video and CT-derived virtual bronchoscopic views. Experiments drawing on bronchoscopic video sequences demonstrate that DUNEDepth more accurately derives depth information and camera motion trajectories than previously proposed self-supervised methods.

Abstract: Diabetic macular edema (DME) is a leading cause of severe vision loss among working-age adults. Although intravitreal anti-vascular endothelial growth factor (anti-VEGF) injections currently serve as the first-line therapy for DME, a notable subset of patients fails to respond despite the substantial cost and clinical burden, suggesting they may benefit more from alternative interventions. Automated, noninvasive pre-treatment response assessment could therefore spare non-responders from the current trial-and-error treatment paradigm. In this study, we propose a novel conditional adversarial diffusion framework that synthesizes post-treatment optical coherence tomography (OCT) images from baseline scans, enabling the derivation of each patient's initial response to anti-VEGF therapy. The model was developed with subject-level 5-fold cross-validation on a US cohort (n = 127) and externally validated on an independent Iranian cohort (n = 44). Benchmarked against fourteen baseline and state-of-the-art translation models, the proposed model achieved the best treatment-response prediction performance among the methods evaluated for that task on both cohorts, with balanced accuracies of 85.7% and 93.0% on the US cohort and 85.2% and 95.3% on the external Iranian cohort at the 10% and 20% central subfield thickness reduction thresholds, respectively. Ultimately, this framework offers a decision-support tool to help ophthalmologists anticipate initial anti-VEGF outcomes in DME prior to treatment initiation, toward personalized therapy selection. The source code will be made publicly available upon acceptance.

</details>

Abstract: A brain MRI scan can carry an unknown mix of artifacts, so restoration has to be blind, yet most deep-learning methods are built to correct one known artifact. We present Dx-Rx, an agent in which a multimodal large language model (MLLM) measures suspected artifacts before it repairs them. To our knowledge, it is the first agent to restore whole 3-D MRI volumes, whereas earlier MRI agents restore 2-D slices. In a loop, it looks at slices of the 3-D volume, calls five diagnostic tools (noise, spike, ghosting, bias field, motion) and four restoration tools in any order it chooses, checks each repair, can return to any earlier version to change the order, and ends with a radiology-style report of its diagnostic reasons, the repairs it applied and their results. We simulated up to five artifacts on 148 whole 3-D T1-weighted volumes, at levels calibrated to radiologists' grades of real scans. An MLLM that only looked at the image, as earlier MRI agents do, removed at most 19.7 % of the artifact error. With the best of five MLLMs, Dx-Rx removed a mean 64.5 %, nearly matching the 64.6 % of the same tools given the ground-truth artifacts, which are never known for a clinical scan. With all five models, it made no volume worse and was on par with or better than an expert rule-based pipeline built from the same tools (62.3 %). Code and demo are public at https://itu-biai.github.io/measure-before-you-repair.

</details>

Abstract: Non-line-of-sight (NLOS) imaging aims to sense objects outside the direct line of sight. While most previous work focuses on the canonical around-the-corner configuration, this work considers a more challenging occlusion-based NLOS setting in which targets are hidden by scene geometry. To avoid the challenge of collecting large real-world datasets, we have developed a photon-based Monte Carlo simulator to generate diverse synthetic training data and validate against experimental data. We trained an AI model to identify multi-bounce photon patterns associated with hidden targets. The model achieves an Average Precision (AP) of 0.91 on synthetic test data and, when transferred directly to 180 real measurements acquired with a TMF8829 SPAD flash LiDAR, achieves 86.67% accuracy and an AP of 0.95. These results show that simulated transient data can support hidden-target detection with a low-cost commercial sensor and provide an initial step toward more general occlusion-based NLOS sensing and, ultimately, hidden-scene reconstruction.

</details>

Abstract: Improving learned LiDAR compression increasingly relies on heavier inference, making it harder to meet the constraints of real-time embedded systems. We present ELiCv2, which removes two sources of redundant computation in progressive coding. Its discrete entropy interface selects a precomputed integer CDF instead of materializing one from a per-voxel distribution, while its occupancy-driven kernel-map builder derives finer-level sparse-convolution kernel-maps directly from the decoded hierarchy, eliminating coordinate-based neighbor search. ELiCv2 reinvests the recovered computation budget in lightweight ego-motion-aware temporal conditioning that gathers local occupancy context from the aligned previous reconstruction. On four LiDAR datasets and an NVIDIA Jetson Thor, ELiCv2 achieves a mean BD-rate reduction of 30.97% over G-PCC with channel width C=32 while sustaining the 20Hz nuScenes capture rate. Increasing the channel width to C=64 improves the mean reduction to 35.22%.

</details>

Abstract: Reconstructing three-dimensional (3D) coronary anatomy from sparse angiographic views could support percutaneous coronary intervention, but limited angular coverage and imperfect vessel segmentations introduce substantial geometric ambiguity. We propose a vessel-specific reconstruction framework combining a learned 3D diffusion prior, visual-hull initialization, and projection-based data consistency. The unconditional diffusion model is trained on coronary vessel masks without paired 2D--3D data and reused across different view counts without retraining. During inference, a scaled visual hull initializes reconstruction, while projection-based updates constrain diffusion refinement using patient-specific vessel observations. We evaluated the framework on simulated angiograms derived from ImageCAS computed tomography angiography volumes using two, four, and eight views at two contrast levels. Across 50 evaluation coronary-tree cases, the proposed method achieved higher mean Dice and clDice than visual hull, deep image prior (DIP), and hash-grid implicit neural representation (INR) at all view counts under reduced contrast. With eight views, improvements over DIP, the best-performing baseline at lower contrast, were approximately 0.13 in Dice and 0.22 in clDice. At higher contrast, performance remained competitive with hash-grid INR, the best-performing eight-view baseline. The preferred diffusion starting timestep depended on observation quality and view count, with excessive refinement reducing accuracy at higher contrast. Projection guidance improved both metrics across all conditions. Averaging stochastic reconstructions further improved accuracy, while sample disagreement localized errors with average-precision gains of 0.14--0.21 over error prevalence. These findings support diffusion-prior reconstruction from sparse vessel observations, although clinical validation remains necessary.

</details>

Abstract: Robust Principal Component Analysis (RPCA) is essential in signal processing, with applications in hyperspectral imaging, face recognition, and video background modeling. Traditional RPCA methods often suffer from insufficient utilization of data priors and struggle to capture complex image structures. Conversely, advanced deep denoisers like DnCNN, FFDNet, and DRUNet excel at leveraging image data priors but lack physical model constraints and demonstrate poor robustness against structured noise in RPCA tasks. This paper proposes a novel Plug-and-Play (PnP) method for multi-channel RPCA, integrating both the physical generative model and cutting-edge deep priors. Extensive experiments on RGB and hyperspectral datasets show that PnP-PDA achieves better quantitative performance than conventional RPCA methods and state-of-the-art methods, including deep denoisers and tensor RPCA approaches, while preserving visual details.

</details>


### [409] [Label-Preserving Fourier Domain Adaptation with Generative-AI and Real Style Donors for 3D Mitral Valve Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05106)
*Javeria Javed,Ahmed Salim,Muhammad Mahboob Ur Rahman*

Abstract: Accurate segmentation of the mitral valve from three-dimensional transesophageal echocardiography (3D TEE) is severely restricted by the scarcity of publicly available expert- annotated volumetric datasets. To address this critical limitation, we investigated Fourier Domain Adaptation (FDA), utilizing general-purpose generative-AI images (from platforms like Chat- GPT, Google Gemini, and Microsoft MAI) as annotation-free style donors for 3D mitral valve segmentation. We employed a DeepMitral residual U-Net model on the public MVSeg2023 dataset, comparing six conditions over three runs: real-only training, traditional augmentation, FDA with each generative source, and a non-AI FDA control using real-image style donors, plus an optimization-matched real-only control and a \b{eta} sensitivity analysis. Case-level paired testing showed that FDA with these generative sources led to significant reductions in boundary- and surface-related errors across all three donor conditions relative to the primary real-only baseline. However, the impact on the core segmentation metric, Generalized Dice Score (GDS), proved generator-dependent. ChatGPT-FDA showed a significant GDS advantage over the optimization-matched control but not over the primary baseline, and Gemini-FDA showed no significant GDS difference. Notably, only MAI-FDA provided a statistically robust and significant increase in GDS compared to the primary baseline. The \b{eta} sensitivity was not robust across training runs. These findings confirm that FDA remains an important augmentation mechanism. While generative-AI images offer a practical means of acquiring style diversity without new annotations, the non-AI control performed comparably to ChatGPT-FDA, so we do not conclude that this approach is superior to training with real-image donors.

</details>

Abstract: Quantitative MRI methods can offer tissue parameter maps (e.g. T1) that are comparable across scanners, sessions and subjects, together with multiple contrast-weighted images from a single scan. However, most data are acquired at 1 mm, which limits their use in neurodegenerative diseases that affect fine brain structures. We introduce, to our knowledge, the first diffusion-model-based reconstruction for quantitative MRI at 0.5 mm isotropic resolution from scans of less than 5 minutes, which has eightfold smaller voxel volume than clinical protocols. The key idea is to apply the prior to the synthesized contrast images rather than to the subspace coefficients. The prior is then independent of the temporal basis and the sequence, which improves generalization and allows a 2-D multiscale energy (MuSE) model trained on grayscale CelebA face images to be used without any MRI training data. The explicit energy makes reconstruction MAP estimation by a step-size-free majorize-minimize algorithm that regularizes a rotating subset of contrasts along different axes, which makes 3-D multi-contrast reconstruction tractable. For 3-D radial MPnRAGE at 7 T in 4.3 min, MuSE reduced the phantom NRMSE from 0.133 (CG-SENSE) and 0.091 (L1-wavelet) to 0.074 and the WM T1 interquartile range from 117 and 47 ms to 38 ms, and it was demonstrated in vivo at 7 T.

</details>

Abstract: Zero echo time (ZTE) MRI is near-silent, but dead time gaps, head motion, and undersampling jointly degrade reconstruction. To develop an accelerated ZTE MRI reconstruction framework that jointly estimates the 3D image and rigid-motion parameters, this work introduces Energy-based Motion Correction (EnMoCo), a regularized maximum a posteriori (MAP) formulation that also compensates for missing k-space center data and angular undersampling artifacts. Solved by alternating between k-space rigid-motion updates and conjugate-gradient data-consistency updates, the framework incorporates regularization via a pre-trained Multi-Scale Energy-based model (MuSE) implemented in a data-efficient 2.5D form. Evaluations on simulated and in vivo 3D ZTE data, spanning dead time gaps of 1.5 to 5 samples, rotations up to 17 degrees and translations up to 10 mm, demonstrate that EnMoCo significantly improves PSNR and SSIM relative to CG-SENSE and Autofocusing baselines (achieving roughly 10 to 15 dB higher PSNR for gaps >= 3.5) while reducing motion blur and tissue contrast inconsistencies.

</details>
